# Changelog

All notable changes to Applye are documented here.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/).
Applye is pre-1.0, so it follows `0.x` versioning: while on `0.x`, the minor
number covers new capability and the patch number covers fixes and release
plumbing. The version in `package.json`, `Cargo.toml`, and `tauri.conf.json`
is the single source of truth; this file tracks what changed at each tag.

## [Unreleased]

### Security

- **The second wave of dependency advisories is closed, and none of it ever shipped.** Nine Dependabot alerts opened against `undici`, plus seven more that only `npm audit` reported - the audit found 16 where Dependabot found 9. All of them are development scope: `undici` reaches the tree through `@angular/build` and through `node-gyp` under `@angular/cli`, `hono` and `brace-expansion` the same way. `npm audit --omit=dev` read 0 before the fix and reads 0 after it, so nothing vulnerable was ever in the Tauri bundle or the web build. Every fix is a patch-level bump forced through `overrides`, because `@angular/build` pins `undici` to the exact string `7.28.0` and npm's own suggested remedy was a semver-major **downgrade** of `@angular/build`. `undici` goes to 6.28.0 and 7.29.0, `hono` to 4.13.0, and the three existing `brace-expansion` pins move up within their own majors to 1.1.18, 2.1.4 and 5.0.9 - keyed by major, which is what keeps the earlier refusal intact: forcing every copy to 5.x breaks the `minimatch` 3.1.5 callers and no gate catches it, so the 1.x copies were left on 1.x and checked by hand to still export a callable. `npm audit` now reports 0 vulnerabilities. The one remaining alert, `glib` RUSTSEC-2024-0429, is unchanged and still not fixable here - the gtk-rs 0.18 stack reaches it through the `gtk` version Tauri pins, it is Linux-only, and it is deliberately left open rather than dismissed so that it is what tells us Tauri has moved.

### Fixed

- **The tracker report's failure line was printed where the export cannot see it, and only opening the route in a browser could find that.** Closing the audit's four leftovers produced one new defect, and it was hiding behind exactly the check that had been deferred three times. `styles.scss` hides `body.printing-report *` and re-shows **only** `app-tracker-report` and its descendants; the "report columns could not be read" line was rendered by `app-tracker-report-print`, one level **above** that subtree. It showed on screen and was invisible in the exported PDF - the single artifact it was written for, produced by a hidden window with no toast and no console. The sheet now takes a `notice` input and prints it on the paper itself. Verified in a real browser: the element resolves inside `app-tracker-report`, in `rgb(180, 35, 24)`, carrying the translated sentence. **`nx build desktop` also caught what `tsc --noEmit` did not** - `[notice]="columnsError()"` passes `null` into a `string` input, and template type-checking only runs in the Angular compiler, so a type-check alone is not a substitute for the build.
- **The remaining three audit leftovers are closed.** `settings.rs`'s factory reset discarded every error from `DELETE FROM sqlite_sequence` because "no such table" is the expected answer on a schema with no AUTOINCREMENT - so a locked or corrupt database was discarded too, inside a transaction that goes on to commit and report success. The expected case is named; nothing else is silent. The quick-view's error hint has its colour, through a new `ui-error-text` utility in `libs/ui` rather than a rule in a stylesheet that is 537 lines against a budget of 400: five stylesheets already declare their own `.status--error`, and `_profile-shell.scss` records that `status`, `status--error`, `muted` and four more are generic names defined **with different values** across eight files, so the global layer deliberately does not claim that name. Verified empirically that the utility reaches inside emulated encapsulation, on a probe carrying a component's own `_ngcontent` attribute. And the **nine raw English operational strings are in the i18n system**: ten keys across all seven locales, each a whole sentence rather than a fragment glued to a tail, because the six non-English locales do not share word order. `TranslateService` is injected into the five hosts that lacked it, following `TailoringService`'s existing precedent in the same layer. `baselineFor` stays pure and returns a **code** (`'missing' | 'unreadable'`) with the CV id instead of a sentence, because a pure function has no injector and could only ever produce English. The parity suite enforces that every locale resolves exactly `en`'s keys **and** that none of them is left as the English text.

- **Eight places swallowed a failure and substituted a value that passed for a real answer.** An audit of every `catch`, `unwrap_or*` and `let _ =` in the repository separated the honest guards from the lies. The honest ones stay: a JSON parser over a stored TEXT column has a defined answer for bad input, and `job-identity`, `archetype`, `geo-scope`, `cv-content` and the four `scoring.utils` parsers are all that shape. The eight below were not that. **Three produced wrong output rather than no output.** `CvGapDialogService.analyze` returned `[]` for a missing key, a dead network and a spent quota alike, so a CV was generated with no gap questions and nothing said - it returns `{ok, questions} | {ok: false, error}` now, generation still never blocks, and only the failure is reported. `FinalChecksService.documentText` returned the **raw `contentJson`** when a document would not parse, and JSON syntax is long and full of words, so the length floor and the keyword overlap both cleared it and the step reported a confident `pass` on a document it had never read; it returns `''` and names the document in `unreadableDocuments`. `baselineFor` fell back from an unreadable base CV to the profile silently, so three tailoring passes rewrote the profile while the wizard still named the selected CV - the fallback stays, and now says which document the result was actually built from. **Four were honestly empty but silent**: the ATS check's failure reached `console.warn` only, a fail-soft job load looked identical to an empty screen, the jobs page swallowed its own follow-up failure, and an unparseable `columns` argument printed a column-less report straight into the user's PDF. **One was a fire-and-forget write** whose failure left a document stuck in draft with nothing pointing at it. On the Rust side two `let _ =` bookkeeping writes in `discover.rs` now log rather than discard, because a lost `last_scan_at` looks like a scan that never ran. **A test was passing because of one of these bugs**: `final-checks.service.spec.ts` built its CV fixture as `{summary: text}`, which is not a `CvContent` at all - `cvContentToMd` threw on it, the catch handed the raw JSON back, and the checks passed over JSON syntax. The fixture is a real CV now, and an unreadable one is asserted separately. Sixteen tests; `application` 1483 to 1493, `desktop` 966 to 969 including a first spec for the print-report route.

- **A failed interview-stage read offered to log the "first" stage for an application that already had several.** Found while writing the rendered check for the fix below, and it is worse than that fix described. `showQuickAdd` gated on `stageSummary() === null`, and a failed read leaves that null exactly as an application with no stages does - so the quick-view did not merely draw a wrong empty state, it raised the mini form that writes a stage, on an application whose stages it had failed to read. Accepting it would have written a duplicate first stage. **An unknown is not a zero**, and the gate now says so: it requires `!stagesError()` as well, because the form's precondition is "this application has 0 stages" and the only evidence for that is a read that came back. The panel gained the branch that was missing underneath it - a failed read prints its reason where the "no stages yet" hint would have gone, and that hint is no longer reachable on a failure. Four tests, including the control case that a genuinely empty read still offers the form. **The danger colour is deliberately not applied**: `quick-view-modal.component.scss` is 537 lines against a budget of 400, so it may not grow until a section is extracted, and the class is left in place as the seam with the reason written into the template.

- **The quick-view stepper claimed an interview had no stages while a toast said the read had failed.** The second half of the fallback audit - defaults standing in for values that were never fetched, and un-awaited promises - came back almost clean, and the reason is systemic: `app.config.ts` installs `provideBrowserGlobalErrorListeners()`, so a floating rejection reaches `ToastErrorHandler` and raises a toast rather than vanishing. That backstop is why the `?? []` / `|| ''` sweep found nothing real and why the six `try`/`finally` blocks in the tracker, regeneration and identity services are contracts rather than swallows - each is documented as propagating to an awaiting caller. **One site was genuinely wrong.** `QuickViewStore.refreshStages` was a `try`/`finally` with no `catch`, called as `void refreshStages(...)` from an effect: the rejection surfaced as a bare global toast, but `stages` was left empty, and an empty list is exactly how the stepper draws "no stages yet". The two halves of one failure disagreed on screen - the panel said the interview had no stages, the toast said something else. It reports like `loadComments` in the same store now, which had the right shape all along: a boolean return, a `stagesError` signal, and a caller that toasts with context. Two tests. **`describeOtherJob`'s `.catch(() => null)` was considered and deliberately left**: the cross-job confirm hides the name behind `@if (crossJobLabel())` and its doc comment already reasons about a job it cannot name, so the degrade is designed rather than accidental.

- **Four tests were describing a version of the app that never shipped, and removing a workaround is what exposed them.** Eleven files in `libs/application` took a parsing or layout function as a **parameter**, because the function used to live in `apps/desktop` and a library may not import the app. That stopped being true when the CV utilities moved down, so the parameters went - and with them the mocks four spec files had been injecting through them. Three of those mocks were lying. An identity "normalizer" stub hid the `personal_details` section that `normalizeCvContent` inserts at order 0 on **every real load**, so three expectations described a document shape the application has never produced. A fake `mergeSection` **appended** a regenerated section where the real one rewrites in place - a test that would have passed against a merge that appended forever. And two tests asserted nothing but "the stub I passed in was called". Those two are now behaviour tests over the real function: a CV saved before skills gained groups holds `items: string[]`, the editor binds to groups, and without the migration that repair performs, such a CV renders empty and is then **saved back emptied** - which is what the tests now pin. No user-visible change; the count is unchanged at 1096 in `application` because the two seam-only tests were replaced rather than deleted. **A test seam is only worth keeping while it tells the truth**, and this one had quietly stopped.

- **A cover letter generated from a JSON array filled the editor with nothing and said it had worked.** Reading a cover-letter skill's answer was an inline lambda on the page - `JSON.parse(cleanJsonText(text)) as Partial<CoverLetterContent>` - and the cast was the whole validation. An answer that parsed into an **array** or a bare scalar satisfied `JSON.parse`, satisfied the cast, and reached the editor as a letter with no fields, which is indistinguishable on screen from a model that had nothing to say. `CoverLetterGenerateStore` already had the right answer for this - a `bad-json` outcome carrying a truncated excerpt of what the model actually sent - but it could only reach it if the parse threw, and this parse did not. The lambda is now `parseCoverLetterResponse` in `libs/core`, which rejects anything that is not a JSON object, so the silent empty letter becomes the failure message that path was built to show. It mirrors the guard `parseCvSkillResponse` has had in the same file all along. **A truncated answer is still not repaired**, unlike the CV parse: that is what the page did before, and changing what the user sees on a cut-off answer is a product decision rather than a rider on moving a parse across a layer boundary - the reason is written into the function so the inconsistency is not "fixed" by accident later. Six tests; `libs/core` goes 453 to 459 on the same 25 suites.

- **GA4 collected nothing since launch, because the queue was pushed an array where gtag.js only reads an `arguments` object.** `AnalyticsService.load()` built its shim as `(...args) => win.dataLayer?.push(args)` - a plain array per command. gtag.js recognises a queued command only as an `arguments` object and silently ignores anything else, so `js` and `config` never executed, no destination was ever configured, and not one `/g/collect` request left the page. **Every visible sign said it worked**: the `googletagmanager.com/gtag/js?id=G-ZY158GV42C` script loaded, `window.gtag` was a function, `window.google_tag_manager['G-ZY158GV42C']` existed, and `dataLayer` filled to five entries. Nothing threw, nothing warned, no CSP was involved - the only symptom was GA4's own "data collection isn't active on your website", which reads like a tag that is missing rather than one that is present and mute. Confirmed on the live site rather than reasoned about: with the consent banner accepted, zero requests to `google-analytics.com`; then a canonical `function g(){dataLayer.push(arguments)}` plus `js`, `config` and one event on the same page produced a `region1.google-analytics.com/g/collect` hit immediately. The shim is now `installGtag()`, an exported function that pushes `arguments`, which is also what makes it testable - the service's own `load()` is unreachable in tests because the committed measurement ID is the placeholder that keeps checkouts out of the real property. Four tests pin the queued value's type, its contents, that an existing `dataLayer` is reused, and that the shim is published on the window; the first of them fails against the array form. `prefer-rest-params` is disabled on that one line with the reason written above it, because obeying the lint rule is the bug.

- **The fifth icon-button copy folded, and the ratchet found the seam again.** `interview-prep-detail`'s `.ipd__icon-btn` is gone. Its base rule was an **exact** `variant="secondary"` - same 28px box, same `--border-default` border, same `--text-secondary`, same hover pair - so the fold is a deletion rather than a reconciliation, and the single accepted difference is `:disabled`, 0.35 against the design system's 0.5. The danger modifier maps cleanly only because the destructive variant is quiet at rest again; before that fix this fold would have been a second visible regression. The template was **311 against a budget of 300**, and the fold costs +2 lines per button, so the file-size gate refused it and `interview-stage-actions/` came out - the four move/edit/delete controls of one stage, template **311 -> 274** and stylesheet 363 -> 346. Deliberately the narrow cut: the wider `.ipd__actions` cluster contains `.ip__pop`, which lives in `_ip-shared.scss` and reaches the page through a `@use` a child component would not inherit - the trap that has now cost this campaign four components, seen in advance for the first time. The buttons sit directly in a flex row, so the host element is `display: contents` and does not become the flex item; that shape is what cost Profile its paired-field row. **Checked on a rendered screen before merge**, including the hover: twelve buttons at 28.00 x 28.00, the first stage's up and the last stage's down disabled, delete red with a tint only under the pointer.

- **The icon button was never square, and deleting became red before anyone pointed at it.** The first rendered check of the fold that put every icon button on `appButton size="icon"` found two things no gate in this repository can see. The box was **28 x 29.84**: the icon sits in an inline `<svg>` inside a `<lucide-icon>` wrapper, so the wrapper is a line box and reserves descender space below the baseline - 1.84px, whatever the icon measures. Eight of the nine call sites use a 13-16px icon and were saved by the 28px minimum; the ninth is the topbar theme toggle at 18px, which is the very button the change was written around, and the comment in the stylesheet asserting that every call site is 13-16px was counted rather than recalled and found to be wrong. A 16px icon cleared 28 by **0.16px**. `.btn--icon svg { display: block }` takes the icon out of the line box, and the box is square at every size, including sizes past 28. Separately, `variant="danger"` colours at rest, while all four page rules it replaced were `:hover`-only - and the variant had **zero consumers** before that fold gave it two, so the pages' behaviour was the one with evidence and the variant was the untested claim. A document row went from one tone to three: ghost at `--text-secondary`, the export control still at `--text-tertiary`, and delete at `--danger`. Destructive icons are quiet at rest again and red when pointed at, and the export control - a `<label>`, which the directive cannot reach, which is why it was left behind - is hand-matched to its neighbours. Neither defect is reachable from type-check, lint, the suite, either build, or `quality:style-move`; a rendered screen is the only instrument, for the second time in this campaign.

- **Discover's "scope" link has navigated nowhere since the Sources drawer was extracted.** The drawer's summary line offers `<a routerLink="/settings">` so the user can change what a scan covers, but `RouterLink` stayed behind in the page's `imports` when that markup moved into a child component. Without the directive the attribute is literal: no `href`, no navigation, and a link that looks exactly like a link. Nothing failed - `routerLink` is a valid attribute name, so type-check, lint and the whole suite passed on it, and the only warning the compiler raised was about the page's now-unused import rather than the drawer's missing one. This is the same shape as the host-element trap the last three extractions hit, one layer up: markup that moves needs its **directives** as well as its rules to move with it. The drawer imports `RouterLink` now, the page no longer does, and a test asserts the anchor's `href` - which is the only visible difference between the two states.

- **Profile's paired fields stopped filling their row, and the labels were below contrast.** Extracting the contact fields into `profile-text-field` put a host element between `.field-row` and `.field`, so the flex item became `<app-profile-text-field>` while the `flex: 1` that makes a pair share the row stayed on the `.field` inside it - inert, because its parent is no longer the flex container. The fields fell back to sizing themselves from their content: first name and last name measured **173.5px each in an 846px row**, leaving the right half of the card empty. The rule now names the two shapes a row's children actually take, `.field-row > .field` and `.field-row > app-profile-text-field`, with `min-width: 0` so a long email shrinks in place instead of pushing the row past the card. Both fields measure 419px. Separately, `.field__label` was `--text-tertiary`, which computes to **3.13:1** on the card - under the 4.5:1 a label that small needs; it is `--text-secondary` at 5.4:1 now. jsdom performs no layout, so the widths cannot be asserted in a unit test; the shape can, and there is a test that fails if a `.field-row` child is anything the stylesheet does not give `flex` to.

- **A key check that answered for the wrong provider could unlock the wizard.** Onboarding asks the OS keyring whether the selected provider already has a key, and that lookup is asynchronous. Switching provider while it was in flight let the answer for the provider you left land on the one you had moved to - so a provider with no key could report as connected, and Continue unlocked on a key that was not there. The guard against this existed but nothing tested it: removing it left all 1224 tests green. It is now covered, along with the key-format check and the path where the keyring write fails.

### Added

- **The triage canon is now tool-independent, and the four other tools this repository is worked on from carry pointers to it.** The system shipped a day earlier was written from inside Claude Code and leaked it: the rubric lived beside Claude's model names, and `.cursor/rules/100-aif-model-router.mdc` still described three unnamed tiers for a skill that had been deleted. **One canon, five thin pointers.** `docs/ai/model-policy.md` keeps the rubric, the thresholds, the adjustments and the gate commands, and its routing table is now by **role** - cheapest, mid, frontier - because the names differ per tool. Each tool's own instruction file carries a pointer plus that tool's names: `CLAUDE.md` and the global skill, `AGENTS.md` for Codex, `.cursor/rules/100-task-triage.mdc` for Cursor, `.github/copilot-instructions.md` for Copilot. Copying the rubric into five files is how five files come to disagree, and the disagreement stays invisible until two agents score the same task differently. **Enforcement is stated per tool rather than assumed**, because it genuinely differs: Claude Code's `UserPromptSubmit` hook and Cursor's `alwaysApply: true` are deterministic; Codex reads `AGENTS.md` once per session, so placement is the gate and a per-prompt event stays **unverified**; Copilot has no known mechanism and is marked **advisory** in its own file; Antigravity has no rules file at all yet, only `.antigravity/mcp.json`. **Only Claude Code's model names are marked verified.** Everywhere else the row says unverified and the verdict reports the role, to be filled in the first time work actually happens from that tool by reading its own list - nothing fails when a model name is wrong, the wrong model is simply selected. Two stale references to the deleted `aif-model-router` were removed from the Cursor rules while doing this, and the Copilot instructions turned out to carry a **stale Rust budget of 800 lines** against the governance figure of 500 plus 600 counted separately for inline tests, with no mention of the 250-line store budget or the gateway rule at all - all three corrected.

- **Every task now gets scored before it gets a model.** A `task-triage` skill runs first on any request and prints a verdict: a 0-10 score across five axes - blast radius, ambiguity, risk, verification, unknowns, 0-2 each - and then the model, the reasoning effort, the subagent plan and the token budget that score justifies. `docs/ai/model-policy.md` is the local table it reads, rewritten from three unnamed tiers (`fast`, `standard`, `deep`) to named models, effort levels and thresholds; `aif-model-router` is deleted, because a router with no model names, no effort levels, no score and no way to tell whether it had been applied was advice rather than a gate. **Three rules earn it its place:** escalate _context_ before escalating the model, because a cheap model with the right three files beats a frontier model with none; repeated failure means the diagnosis is wrong rather than the model too small; and ambiguity scored 2 goes to `aif-grilling` before any edit whatever the total. **Delegation stays opt-in** - triage always prints, and spawns nothing unless asked, because autonomous fan-out is how a 3/10 task becomes a 200k-token session with the spend invisible until it has happened. The triage itself is bound by the same discipline: it scores from the request text and reads no files, because reading files to produce an estimate is the work rather than the estimate. Enforcement is a `UserPromptSubmit` hook rather than a sentence in `CLAUDE.md`, since an instruction is read once and drifts out of attention across a long session; the hook stays silent for prompts under 15 characters and for plain continuations, so the reminder does not become noise. Model names for Codex, Cursor and Antigravity are deliberately **absent** and described by role instead - the same rule this repository already applies to lucide icon identities, and for the same reason: nothing fails, the wrong thing is simply selected.

- **The cover letter's body paragraphs left the page, and `.icon-btn` turned out not to be one rule.** The last block in the editor's template - the paragraph list with its Style, Regenerate and Delete row - is now `cover-letter-body-paragraphs/`, taking `cover-letter-detail.component.html` from **324 to 222 lines against a budget of 300**. That closes a campaign that started at 669. The cut had been blocked for two pull requests on `.icon-btn`, which the previous entries described as one shared class duplicated across four stylesheets. **It is not.** Four files define the name and three define different rules: `cv-list` and `my-jobs` are an identical 28px pair, `cv-detail` is that box with `:disabled` and `--active` instead of a danger modifier, and the cover letter's is 32px with a transition and different colour tokens. Promoting it to a global partial was therefore never a deduplication - it was a three-way reconciliation that would visibly change two other pages, which is why the block's single delete button carries a locally-named `.clb__icon-btn` instead, the call `interview-prep-detail` already made with `.ipd__icon-btn`. The real fix is recorded rather than done: `libs/ui`'s `appButton` already ships `size="icon"` and `variant="danger"`, so all four copies are a reimplementation of a primitive that exists, and folding them onto it is its own change because it is a visible one.

- **Four page stylesheets stopped reimplementing a design-system button, and the gate that blocked the change is what found three real seams.** `.icon-btn` was declared in `cv-detail`, `cv-list`, `my-jobs` and `cover-letter-detail` - and used by `cover-letter-list` through a Sass `@import` nobody could see from either of its own files. All of them now use `appButton size="icon"`, which `libs/ui` has shipped all along. The design system was pinned to the pages rather than the reverse (`.btn--icon` becomes a 28px-minimum square at `--radius-input`), so the fold is a deletion instead of a restyle - defensible only because `size="icon"` had exactly one consumer beforehand. **Converting a class to three attributes costs +2 lines per button, and the file-size ratchet refused the commit** on three files that were already over budget. The response was extraction, not an exception, and it produced better work than the sweep alone: `libs/ui/src/styles/_button.scss` takes `global.scss` from **415 to 345**; `document-row-actions/` - the duplicate/export/delete controls that `cv-list` and `cover-letter-list` rendered byte for byte identically - takes `cv-list.component.html` from **311 to 283**, under budget for the first time; and `cv-section-actions/` takes `cv-detail.component.html` from **492 to 466**. Two visual deltas are deliberate: the topbar theme toggle goes 34px to 28px, and ghost icons sit one contrast step higher.

- **`$any(...)` was hiding a type hole in both document lists.** They read the chosen export format as `$any($event.target).value` and handed it to a handler typed `'docx' | 'pdf'`, so any string would have gone through. Extracting the markup made the compiler object, because an output has to declare what it emits; there is a type guard now, and an unrecognised value is dropped rather than forwarded.

- **The Availability and salary card now says what it does with your answers.** Filling in an earliest start date, a salary expectation or a notice period changes nothing visible: the three are inputs to the AI prompt, which states them in the final body paragraph, so they reach the letter only on the next generate or regenerate and never appear in the preview as lines of their own. That is deliberate, and it was undocumented anywhere the user could see - the card looked broken to the person who filled it in. A region-independent hint under the card now says so, in all six locales, distinct from the existing German-market explainer above it, which answers a different question (why a market expects these answers, not what the card does with them). Two tests keep them apart, because an assertion that "a hint exists" would have passed either way.

- **Five cover-letter fields have been rendering as native browser inputs since they were extracted, and the audit that was supposed to catch it said they were clean.** `cover-letter-block/` - date, subject, greeting, closing and signature - took `.coverdetail__full { width: 100% }` with it and left behind the nine declarations that `.coverdetail input:not(...)` had been supplying: background, border, radius, colour, font, size, padding and focus. It is the exact trap ADR-0005 amendment sixteen was written for, in the one component the amendment's own retrospective audit skipped: it checked the style card and the popover, both extracted in the same pull request, and did not check the third. The block now carries its own copy keyed on `.coverdetail__full`, and a test fails if the markup stops matching it. **Reported from the running app by the maintainer, not by any gate** - `quality:style-move` passes on it by construction, and it had been merged for two days.

- **The regenerate spinner wobbled instead of spinning.** `<lucide-icon>` is a custom element, so it is `display: inline` by default; an inline box is sized from the line box around it, so `transform-origin: 50% 50%` resolves off the icon's actual centre and a rotation traces an ellipse. It is `inline-flex` now, with an explicit origin, and it picks up the `prefers-reduced-motion` guard that `settings.component.scss` already applies to its own spinner.

- **The paragraph textareas would have rendered as bare browser boxes, and every gate would have passed.** ADR-0005's amendment sixteen says the classes moved markup names are only half the audit - the other half is every descendant selector rooted at the page that reached inside it. Here that was `.coverdetail textarea`, which supplies nine declarations, and **nothing global styles a bare `textarea`** anywhere in the app, so the child had to carry its own copy keyed on `.coverdetail__textarea`. `quality:style-move` cannot see this by construction: the page's rule still exists and still carries all nine, and what changed is only what it can reach. The checklist caught it for the third consecutive extraction, and the compiled bundle confirms the carry landed in the child's own encapsulated scope rather than the page's.

- **The preview toggle has never shown that it was on.** The cover letter's Edit/Preview button bound `[class.icon-btn--active]`, but no `.icon-btn--active` rule is reachable from that page - only `cv-detail` defines one - and the class was being put on a design-system `appButton` that does not use that vocabulary anyway. It now switches the button's own `variant` between `primary` and `secondary`, which is the vocabulary actually rendered. Found while auditing which rules the extraction had to carry.

- **The rule that keeps a component away from the data gateway is now enforced by lint, and it is an error.** ADR-0005 planned to enforce it by removing `type:data` from `type:app`'s allowlist once no component injected `DbService`. **Those are not the same rule.** `depConstraints` keys on the _project_ tag, so that flip also bans the gateway from the app's 18 `shared/*` services - files the ADR never proposed to move - which is why it was unreachable for a reason that had nothing to do with pages. A `no-restricted-syntax` rule over `*.component.ts` says what was meant, and it can ship today: `COMPONENTS_STILL_USING_THE_GATEWAY` in `eslint.config.mjs` names the 26 components that still inject `DbService`, and a component not on that list **fails the build**. The list only shrinks - each migrated page deletes its own line, and when it empties the rule goes with it. This closes the window ADR-0005 called its own known weak point, rather than waiting for the whole service tier to move. Verified in both directions, because a rule that cannot fail is worse than no rule: it errors on a component not listed, stays silent on all 26 that are, and does both under `nx lint` as well as a direct run. **The allowlist entries are double-star globs on purpose** - `nx lint` loads the project's config, which spreads the root one, so a `files` pattern resolves against `apps/desktop` rather than the repository root, and a repo-relative path matches nothing at all. The first version of this list was exactly that: silent, and therefore useless.

- **A check that proves a stylesheet split did not drop anything.** Splitting a page into child components moves CSS between files, and until now that move was verified by comparing which selectors existed before and after. That check cannot fail the way it needs to: a selector can survive with its declarations gone. It did. During the Discover Sources drawer extraction the shared `.dv-input, .dv-select` rule was split so the child kept only `.dv-select` and the page kept `.dv-input,` as a selector with no body - which Sass silently attached to the rule that followed it. Three inputs rendered with browser defaults and the page's filter field inherited a 60vh centred empty-state layout, and type-check, lint, 1224 unit tests and the Angular build all passed on it. `npm run quality:style-move -- --base main <files>` compiles both sides of the split and compares **declarations** per selector, reporting what was lost and what was gained. Run against the broken tree it names `.dv-input` and all eight declarations; against the fixed tree it reports lossless. It is not part of `npm run quality`, because it needs the specific files of the split under review.

### Removed

- **Thirteen generator scaffold files that have been dead since the libraries were created, and one redundant Tauri permission.** `nx g @nx/angular:library` writes a placeholder component per library, and `libs/ui`, `libs/data` and `libs/i18n` still carried theirs: `lib-ui`, `lib-data` and `lib-i18n`, each a four-file set whose template reads `<p>Ui works!</p>` and whose spec asserts the component can be constructed. Nothing rendered any of the three selectors, and `Ui`, `Data` and `I18n` appear in no import outside their own folders - **but `libs/ui/src/index.ts` exported `Ui`**, so it was public API of a library that ships in the desktop bundle. `apps/desktop/src/app/app.scss` went the same way: zero bytes and no `styleUrl` pointing at it since `app.ts` was written. What surfaced them was hashing every `.ts`, `.html` and `.scss` in `apps` and `libs`: the three empty scaffold stylesheets are byte-identical to each other and to `app.scss`, and four identical files with four different names is what a duplicate report is for. Separately, `capabilities/default.json` listed `dialog:allow-open` next to `dialog:default`, and the plugin's own `permissions/default.toml` defines that default set as `["allow-message", "allow-save", "allow-open"]` - the line granted nothing the line above it had not. `opener` is **not** collapsed to its default set for the same reason: listing `allow-open-url` and `allow-default-urls` by hand is what keeps `allow-reveal-item-in-dir` out. Verified with `cargo check`, which re-runs `tauri-build` and fails on a capability that does not resolve.

- **Three Discover row rules that nothing renders.** `.dv-row__preview`, `.dv-row__detailactions` and `.dv-row__original` were left behind when the inline row preview became the full-page detail screen: the markup that named them is gone, and a repo-wide search over every template, class and stylesheet in `apps` and `libs` finds no reference, static or built by string. Forty lines, and `discover.component.scss` goes **938 -> 903** without any user-visible change. `quality:style-move` deliberately was not run: this is a removal, not a move, and the check exists to report exactly the declarations this commit intends to drop.

### Changed

- **Opening a job is a store now, and the extraction caught a reset that was about to lose a flag.** The second of three cuts against `jobs.component.ts`, **723 to 612**. `JobDetailLifecycleStore` (160 lines) owns what entering a job loads and what leaving one throws away: `enterJob`, the per-job reset, the load sequence, and the reconciliation that runs when the document editor sends the user back. `JobTailoringStore` (72) owns the seven-field `TailorContext` that `TailoringService` takes on every call and holds none of - it exists a pull request early on purpose, because `restoreFromCache` runs during a job load, and assembling that record on the page for one more PR would mean assembling it twice. **The router stays on the page**, as `ADR-0005` says: the `paramMap` subscription and `ngOnDestroy` are still there, and the page hands the store a `JobRouteEntry` - three booleans and a string - instead of a query-param map, which is also what lets the store be tested without a `RouterTestingModule`. **The near-miss**: the old reset cleared `cache`, `fromCache`, `stale` and `postTailorSaved` by hand, and `JobScoringService.reset()` covers only the first three. Collapsing the four into one call would have left `postTailorSaved` set, telling the next job its rescore had already been committed to My Jobs. It is cleared explicitly, with the reason written above it, and a test fails if it stops being. 16 tests, including the one that pins the no-blink guarantee: the wizard view is decided **before** the first `await`, so the detail view never paints for a frame before the wizard replaces it.

- **The job detail page's document block is three stores in `libs/application`, and the extraction found a feature with no way to reach it.** `jobs.component.ts` was 977 lines against a budget of 400 - the last page still shaped the way `ADR-0005` was written to end, view and state and orchestration in one class. Its largest coherent block moves out: `JobFinalChecksStore` assembles the `FinalCheckInputs` record that four call sites were building by hand, `JobDocumentDraftsStore` generates a CV or cover letter and raises the gap-fill dialog, and `JobDocumentsStore` decides whether a linked document is still current, commits it to the library, and navigates to its editor. The page is **723 lines**, and the three stores are 72, 172 and 165 against a store budget of 250. **The split into three rather than two was forced by measurement, not taste**: as one class the documents half came to 278 lines, and the seam was already in the call graph - `commit()` calls into the drafts store and nothing calls back. `JobDetailStore` gained `ensureApplicationOrThrow`, because both new stores need an application row and neither can call the other without closing a cycle; the comment claiming that store had no `TranslateService` was wrong and is corrected. **`retailorFromFinalChecks` deliberately stays on the page**: it drives tailoring, rescoring and documents at once, and moving it would drag the tailoring block along before its turn. **The finding**: `CoverLetterTailorService.modalOpen` is set in exactly one place, inside `prepare()`, whose only caller was a page method with no callers of its own - so the "tailor an existing cover letter to this job" modal cannot be opened, and the `startTailoringCoverLetter` bound in the template is only reachable from inside it. Both are moved unchanged rather than deleted, with the reason written above them: whether that flow gets its trigger back or goes away is a product decision, and deleting a working feature because its button is missing would make that decision by default. 24 tests, on a block that had none.

- **Three governance documents still described an allowlist that two amendments had already deleted.** `CLAUDE.md` and `AGENTS.md` told every agent that a component injecting `DbService` fails the build "unless it is named in `COMPONENTS_STILL_USING_THE_GATEWAY`, a list of 1 that only ever shrinks", and `docs/governance/CODE_QUALITY.md` put that list at 26 and stated that `type:data` "is still in `type:app`'s allowlist". None of it is true: `jobs` deleted the last entry in amendment fifty-six and the constant is gone from `eslint.config.mjs`, `type:data` left `type:app` in amendment fifty-five, and the restriction now covers `AiService` and `JobSourceService` as well as `DbService`, over `app.ts` as well as `*.component.ts`. The gap mattered in a specific direction - **the docs described the rule as more permissive than it is**, so an agent reading them would look for the escape hatch, find no constant to add a line to, and have to reconstruct the current rule from the config anyway. All three now say what `eslint.config.mjs` enforces, and `CODE_QUALITY.md` additionally records why the `no-restricted-syntax` rule is kept when the boundary rule strictly subsumes it: nx cannot carry a message naming the ADR, and a gateway re-exported through `@applye/application` would satisfy the tag check with the `inject()` call as the only remaining evidence.

- **The CV preview's header is now its own component, which a decision from August said could not be done.** The preview's page template is one file of nearly 900 lines against a budget of 300, made of eight blocks that draw the parts of a CV. Splitting it was ruled out in August for a concrete reason: every block spoke one shared protocol, so pulling one out meant handing the new component about twenty things to work at all, against a project limit of eleven. That reasoning was right about the file as it stood. Two changes since then turned most of that protocol into services the new component can simply ask for, and re-measuring found the real boundary is six values, all plain data. The header block moved, and the template drops to 779 lines. The mechanism that made it possible is worth stating: the page is assembled by a paginator that places blocks on A4 pages, and it accepts templates rather than components, so a four-line template wrapper stays behind and hands its values to the component. Checked on a real two-page CV, and that check earned its place: **the first attempt shipped the header with none of its styling** - the name rendered in normal case where the design calls for uppercase, the photo lost its float, and the selection ring and field label vanished. The cause is a rule of the framework: a stylesheet only applies to markup declared by its own component, so rules that stay behind when markup moves quietly stop matching. Nothing failed - not the type checker, not the linter, not 3071 tests, not either build, not even the check this project already has for exactly this mistake, which compares stylesheets and correctly reported that no rule had been deleted. Rules a single block owns now live with that block, and rules every block shares live in one file both include. Fixed and re-checked on screen, pagination unchanged. No user-visible change.

- **The worst file in the application is finally inside its size budget, and what got it there was not aiming at the number.** The CV preview class has been the largest file in the app for this whole campaign - 1047 lines against a budget of 400 - and four previous cuts aimed squarely at the number and all missed. This one did not aim at it. The next piece of work needs the preview's header to become a child component, and a child needs two methods that were still sitting on the class: whether a field is currently being text-edited, and what happens when that edit finishes. The alternatives were passing methods around as inputs, which the seven remaining blocks would each have repeated, or letting the child reach back into its parent, which undoes what four changes have spent their effort separating. So edit mode and editor focus became a service of their own, and the file landed at 355 of 400 as a side effect. Twenty-two files remain over budget, down from twenty-three. Five new tests; no user-visible change.

- **The CV preview's selection logic moved out, after a note written in August said the plan was backwards.** The agreed next step was to break the preview's template into child components. A decision recorded on 4 August had already ruled that out and explained why: every block of that template speaks one shared protocol, so a child component would have to be handed about twenty things to work at all, against a project limit of eleven. Checking the header block against today's file confirmed it: sixteen. What had changed since that note was written is that two earlier cuts turned part of the protocol into services a child can simply ask for, leaving one family behind - selection. So the order inverts: selection is not the reward for splitting the template, it is what makes splitting it possible. It now lives in its own service, and the preview class goes 535 lines to 413. The template itself is untouched, deliberately - it calls the moved methods 317 times, and renaming them there would reflow a file that is already three times its size budget and forbidden from growing, so the class keeps a one-line stand-in for each. Those stand-ins are temporary and each disappears with the block that calls it. Sixteen new tests, none of which renders anything - and that they can exist at all is the point, because it is the same freedom the child components will need. No user-visible change.

- **A third piece left the CV preview, and the interesting part is which piece could not.** The largest remaining block was the selection logic - but the preview's template calls it 239 times, and giving those calls a prefix would have pushed eighteen of them past the line-width limit, reflowing a template that is already three times its size budget and forbidden from growing. The template has become the constraint on the class: anything it calls heavily cannot move until the template itself is split. So the work went to a block the template never calls - the code that flattens a CV's sections into the individual blocks the paginator places on pages. It is now a plain function taking what it needs as an argument, which is both smaller than the alternative and the only shape that can be tested without rendering a preview; seven new tests do exactly that, covering photo placement, empty sections, ordering, and the two rules that keep a heading off the bottom of a page and an entry's title with its first bullet. Pagination was checked on screen with a two-page CV.

- **The CV preview gave up its styling rules too, and the slice taken was not the slice planned.** The agreed next step was the selection logic, but that plan had been made against the file as it stood before the previous cut. Measuring again took one command and showed the styling family - every CSS rule the document's fonts, colours, spacing and title lines are built from - to be nearly twice the size, and to depend on almost nothing but the document's own style settings. It moved instead: 816 lines down to 597. A decomposition plan is a hypothesis about a file the previous cut has already changed. Verified on a real CV, where the body text keeps its own colour and the accent colour correctly stays on headings rather than leaking into paragraphs - which is precisely the rule the extracted code exists to enforce.

- **The largest file in the application gave up its editing half.** The CV preview was one class doing three jobs - drawing the document, tracking what the user has selected, and running seventeen inline-edit handlers - at 1047 lines against a 400-line budget. The editing half now lives in its own service beside it: the per-field draft map, the commit rule for every editable part of a CV, and the bold helpers. Which line to cut along came from the existing test files rather than from re-reading the class; they had already drawn a boundary around exactly those handlers. Two methods deliberately stayed behind, the ones that answer "which editor is on screen" - that is a question about the rendered page rather than about a draft. Nothing changes for the user, and that was checked by editing a CV on a real screen rather than by trusting the tests: clicking selects a field, double-clicking opens its editor, typing updates the draft, and leaving the field commits it. 1047 to 816 - still over budget, with the selection half to follow.

- **Two of the project's largest logic files were split along lines they already had.** The analytics computation and the profile-document parser had grown to 665 and 622 lines against a 400-line budget, and neither needed a new idea to divide: the first separates its view types, its seven individual metrics, its date arithmetic, and the function that composes them; the second gives each section of the profile document - education, experience, languages, pay expectation - its own file. The pay-expectation file is named to stay distinct from a same-folder neighbour that reads a **job's** advertised salary, because the two are complementary and identical names would have implied a duplicate. Tests moved with the code they cover, which also took the largest test file off the near-budget list. Nothing changes on screen: these are pure functions with no framework and no input or output, which is why they were chosen to open this stretch of work - the mechanics get proven where a mistake is cheap. Files over their size budget across the repository: 25 to 23.

- **The rule that keeps data access out of components was guarding one service of three, and now guards all of them.** It was written to name the database gateway, which is how three components reaching the **AI** service and one reaching a job-source service went unnoticed until the layer boundary was tightened and the errors read. The three names now come from one list, so a fourth is a single line rather than a second rule. Retiring the rule was the serious alternative - the layer-boundary check now covers strictly more of it - and was rejected for two reasons written into the configuration: that check reports a list of internal tags and cannot be given a message, while this one names the decision record and says where the code belongs, which is the difference between an error a newcomer can act on and one they have to research; and a data service re-exported through the middle layer would satisfy the tag check, leaving the injection itself as the only evidence. The 26-entry allowlist that drove the original migration is deleted, having reached zero. Verified in both directions before merging, as every change to this rule has been.

- **The app can no longer reach the data layer at all, and the count of what stood in the way was wrong by thirty.** The plan said two files still reached the database gateway directly. Flipping the rule and reading the errors said thirty-two - which is the lesson: a blocker list assembled by searching imports is a guess, and making the change is how you check it. Twenty-five of the thirty-two are test files, which stub the gateway their subject needs and are now exempt from this one rule, because a test providing fakes for its subject's collaborators is wiring rather than an architectural dependency. Of the seven real ones, only two were the expected kind. Three more injected the **AI** service, and one a job-source service - a second and third gateway the existing component rule never named, since it only ever guarded the database one. The last was a type-only import that carries no runtime dependency at all; that type moved to the domain library where it belongs. One of the three was injected and **never used**, the second dead injection this campaign has found. The two components that genuinely reached data now delegate to stores: the profile's markdown import and the Paste Job modal, the latter dropping from 245 lines to 128 by keeping only what a modal owns - closing itself, opening the job the store created, and the clipboard read, which stays behind a guard that only fires while the modal is open. **A privacy guard did its job while this moved**: the check that the follow-up drafter contains no way to send mail identifies its subject by file path, so relocating the file made it fail loudly rather than pass over a file that was no longer there. The rule was verified in both directions before merging - a throwaway file importing the gateway is rejected, and all twenty-five tests pass - because a rule that cannot fail is worse than no rule. Ten new tests, all of them coverage that never existed, including the first tests for the clipboard heuristic.

- **The last services left the app's shared folder, and the wall that had held them for two sub-steps was a notification store filed in the wrong place.** The plan said four services had to stop showing their own messages and start returning outcomes for a page to phrase - a real pattern, but not one this needed. `ToastService` is 91 lines of signal state whose only dependencies are Angular signals and the translator, so it was never app-specific; it was a store sitting next to the component that draws it. Moving the store unblocked all four at once, with the drawing half left behind in the app, and **fifty import sites rewritten one-for-one so that no file in the app changed length**. The translator was not a blocker either: it lives in a library the shared layer has always been allowed to use, and twelve files there were already using it, which makes the count of "five blocked by translation" wrong by five. Of the seven services, one ends up genuinely split: the cover-letter tailor was 305 lines against a 250 budget, so its pure text functions went to the domain library and the modal state, the AI pass and its two writes stayed together at 206. **The wizard navigator moved too, against an earlier ruling that it never could.** That ruling was right about the reason and wrong about the unit - only its scroll method touches layout, not the file, and it had four callers rather than the two a first reading found. The navigator now counts scroll requests and the page performs them, which is the general shape for a store that needs something done to the screen. What remains in the shared folder is four component folders, which are user interface and stay there. Nothing changes on screen: counts reconcile in both directions against a baseline taken in a separate checkout - 258 suites and 3028 tests before, 260 and 3033 after, the five new ones covering the extracted scroll helper - and four of the five pull requests were checked on a rendered screen, because a toast that stops appearing breaks no test.

- **Five more services moved into the shared library, and the thing that had been blocking them turned out not to be a dependency at all.** Two of them needed the browser's session storage, and reached it through a handle on the page document - which is what made them look like they depended on the page. They do not: this project decided a year of amendments ago that remembering a preference between screens is not the same as touching the screen, and the same decision applies here unchanged. With those two free, the two files waiting behind them came too. Nothing changes for anyone using Applye: the apply wizard still remembers which step you were on when you step into the document editor and back, which was verified in a real browser three times and compared byte for byte against the version before the change. One more file had to be cut under the library's size limit first, and cutting it exposed that the scoring code wrote the same fourteen fields three separate times by hand - now one builder each, with the one place they deliberately differ written down rather than left to be "tidied up" later. **The remaining seven files in the old folder are now blocked for reasons that are each named**, and one of them is recorded as never moving at all.

- **Sixteen more services moved out of the app and into the shared library, and the plan's own count of them turned out never to have been counted.** The checklist said 22 files were free to move. Reading all 26 of them - rather than trusting the table, which says "seventeen services" and then lists 6 + 4 + 22 - gives 15 free and 11 genuinely stuck, because the old figure only looked at what each file imports directly and ignored what it reaches through its neighbours. Two the plan called stuck were not: the job identity resolver was recorded as depending on a dialog **component**, when what it actually uses is that folder's plain state service, so it and the intake parse behind it came too, and the state service with them. Nothing changes for anyone using Applye. Shipped as three changes rather than one, because one file had to be cut under the library's size limit before it could move at all, and because the two families of files turned out to have no dependency on each other. Test counts were reconciled at every step and the totals never moved - a move is the one change where a green suite proves nothing on its own, and this project has already been bitten by a move that silently deleted the end of a test file. **One hash function had to be renamed on arrival**: the library already had a `coverLetterHashInput` for a different question - which paragraph of the letter needs regenerating - and two different questions sharing one name is exactly how a regeneration silently stops firing.

- **The CV logic now lives where the rest of the app can reach it.** Seven modules - building a CV, rendering it to text, its styling, its page and contact line, and the identity of whatever you click in the live preview - move out of the app and into the shared library. Nothing changes for anyone using Applye. What changes is that **nine files elsewhere no longer have to be handed these functions as arguments** to work around not being allowed to import them, and six services that were stuck behind the same wall can now move too. A seventh module came along that was not in the plan: the styling-scope router turned out to be one of the things those nine files were working around. The test counts were reconciled in both directions - the library gained exactly the seven suites and 152 tests the app lost - because a move is the one change where a green suite proves nothing on its own.

- **The CV content module splits in three, so it can move to where the rest of the app can reach it.** Nothing changes for anyone using the app - this is preparation. The module holds the pure logic behind every CV: building the document, rendering it to text, the contact line, the page margins, and the identity of whatever you click on in the live preview. It sits in the app rather than in a shared library, and **nine files elsewhere already work around that** by having those functions handed to them as arguments. Moving it is blocked by the size rule - the rule treats a moved file as a new one, and this file is 596 lines against a 400 budget - so it was split first: leaf selection and page/contact each become their own file, and the original drops to 352. Consumers are untouched. **The test count caught a real loss during the split**: the suite went green with five tests fewer, because a block had been truncated off the end of a file, and only reconciling the counts found it.

- **The one file the architecture rule could not see is now covered by it, and the number this campaign reported was wrong by one the whole time.** The app's root file is a screen, but it is not _named_ like one - and the rule that stops screens reaching the database matched on the name. So while the list of exceptions counted down from 26 to 0, one file was never on it and never could be. Its database read - which of three things to show when the app opens: the welcome screen, the tour, or the app itself - now lives in a store alongside the code that already recorded the answer, so one layer stops writing what another reads. **It fails open exactly as before:** if that read fails, the app opens normally rather than trapping you outside data that is otherwise fine, and that is a test now instead of a comment. The rule's pattern was widened to cover the file, and its own error message no longer points at an exception list that no longer exists. Verified on a rendered screen: all three opening screens, plus the failure case.

- **No screen reaches the database directly any more, and the base-CV picker stops offering the wrong CVs.** The list of components allowed to talk to the data gateway started at 26 and only ever shrank; the job page held the last entry, and this empties it. Its four data paths - the job and its application row, the profile and settings, the document library, and creating the application draft - move into a store, and the page renders and orchestrates. The rule that enforces this stays and now binds every component without exception. **A bug fell out of the move:** the base-CV list was narrowed to the CVs written in the job's language on the way in, but replaced with the entire library when you came back from editing a document - so after one round trip the picker and the choose-existing dropdown were offering CVs written for other jobs, in other languages. There is one writer and one meaning now. **One ordering change, named because it is real:** the cached score is restored after the job's data is read rather than partway through it. Verified against a stubbed database in the browser: a four-CV library narrows to the two that belong, on both routes in, and the picker on screen lists only those two. The page's class is 980 lines against a budget of 400 - the apply wizard's orchestration is still on it, and that is a separate piece of work.

- **The apply wizard's last two inline steps become their own components, and a setting neither the page nor the step could own gets a home.** The job page's template was 387 lines against a budget of 300, and the size gate refuses to let an over-budget file grow - so the state migration this is all leading to could not add a single binding until the template came down. Two of the wizard's four steps were still page markup: the updated-score step, and the 146-line review-documents step. That second one is four responsibilities at once, so its final-checks card came out as a third component of its own. **The market and language the documents are written for are read by six page methods and written by the two selects that were moving out**, so they now live in a small service the page provides and both sides inject - which also puts the "changing either invalidates the stored checks" rule in one place instead of two. It had been written twice: once in a page method, once spelled out inline in the template. **Two things the plan got wrong, and looking settled both:** a stylesheet block listed as belonging to the documents step is actually bound by the page header, and stayed; and the line estimate assumed thinner components than the outputs required. **Found while checking reachability:** the base-CV picker's mobile grid rule has been dead since that markup moved out two sessions ago - documented rather than fixed, because restoring it changes the mobile layout. Template 387 -> 236, stylesheet 289 -> 186, class 1036 -> 998. All 1432 existing tests pass unchanged, plus 15 new ones. Verified on a rendered screen at both breakpoints, which is where the only defect class that matters here is visible.

- **The job page's five dialogs and its action row become their own components, and a feature turns out to have no way into it.** The last screen still holding its own state had all three files over budget, and the size gate refuses to let an over-budget file grow, so nothing can move until the template comes down. This is the first of two cuts; the lint allowlist does not move in it. Each new component injects the service that already owned its state, and asks the page - with an output - for the things only the page can do: navigate, write page state, run the document commit. **Two style rules would have been stranded and were caught before merge:** `.muted`, which is page-local and which the photo prompt's "no photo yet" line carries, kept in its original source order because that is what zeroes that line's margin; and the modal shell, now duplicated in the two modals that use it, as all eight other modals in this app already do. **Found while mapping it:** "tailor an existing cover letter to this job" cannot be reached - the only method that opens its modal is called from nowhere, and the class the modal's two fields are styled with is declared in another component's stylesheet and has never reached them. Both were moved verbatim rather than fixed, because an extraction's whole value is that behaviour did not change. Template 686 -> 387, stylesheet 493 -> 289, class 1050 -> 1036. All 1417 existing tests pass unchanged, plus 15 new ones.

- **The onboarding wizard's state moves into `libs/application`, and only one component is left injecting the data gateway.** Seven stores now hold what the page used to: the five its steps already had, plus two the page had nowhere else to put - one for the AI-setup step's mode, its settings writes and the dispatch every other store calls through, and one for the profile the wizard must not destroy on a re-run and the CV document it writes. That second one answers with an outcome rather than raising a toast, so "declined to write" and "the write was refused" stay apart even though only the second is worth telling the user about. **The wizard's own state stays the truth for the duration of the wizard**: the dispatch deliberately never reads the settings row back, because the AI step's choices only land there on finish and reading them back sent every in-wizard call to the pre-onboarding defaults. Two shapes were decided by the layer boundary rather than by preference - `libs/application` has no `TranslateService` and no Tauri plugins, so the stores publish i18n keys and URLs and the cards turn them into sentences and open them; and the parse and layout helpers that stay in `apps/desktop` are passed in, the seam the Documents stores already use. `onboarding.component.ts` 573 -> 312, template unchanged at 291. 59 tests moved from `desktop` to `application` and 16 new ones cover the two new stores; the tests whose assertions only mean something against the real layout helper stayed where it is, unchanged.

- **The onboarding wizard's resume and targeting steps become their own components, and three dead style rules come out with them.** The last screen before My Jobs had three files over budget - the class, the template and the stylesheet - and the size gate refuses to let an over-budget file grow, so the state migration could not land until the template and stylesheet came down. This is that change; the lint allowlist does not move in it. The two new step components inject a shared service rather than taking inputs, which is the convention this folder already documents, and the wizard keeps what is its own: opening the file dialog, advancing a step, and deciding which provider its own AI calls go to. **Three rules were found outliving the markup they were written for.** The CLI status icon's green and amber tints had been dead since that panel was extracted months ago - the markup moved, the rules did not, and Angular scopes a stylesheet to its own template; they work again. Four `note` rules that no template uses at all were deleted. And two rules stranded by this extraction were caught only by looking at the screen: the three resume tiles were stacking instead of sitting in a row, and the roles label and its "AI suggested" badge were stacking instead of sharing one. The style gate read clean throughout - it proves a declaration was not lost, never that it still reaches. Template 514 -> 291, stylesheet 642 -> 391. All 1210 lines of the screen's existing tests still pass, plus 14 new ones.

- **Settings' state moves into five stores, and the screen leaves the lint allowlist.** Allowlist 3 -> 2; the class falls 564 -> 277, under its 400 budget for the first time, and no longer injects the database, the keychain or the AI service. The five stores land on the seams the extraction had already cut - the settings row, the provider key, the CLI bridge, the geo target and the connection test - so this change is state movement and nothing else: the five section components and their 30 tests were not touched. **The settings row has exactly one copy**, read by the geo target from the settings store rather than kept twice, because two copies can disagree about what the next save writes. **The key path moved into a store with no read path at all** - stored is a yes/no answer, never the key; the typed draft is cleared the moment a save succeeds and deliberately kept when one fails, so a transient keychain error does not make you retype it. Three things stayed on the page because they are not the layer's: the locale reapplied after changing the UI language, every toast, and the reload after a factory reset - the store wipes the database and the keychain, dropping the window is not its job. **Checked on a rendered screen including the three failure paths**: a failed reset closes its confirmation rather than leaving an armed "delete everything" dialog standing, a failed save releases the button, and a failed geo toggle rolls the row back to exactly what it was.

- **Settings splits into five section components, so its state migration can land at all.** The screen's template was 580 lines against a 300 budget, and the size gate fails an already-over-budget file that grows by even one line - so rebinding the page to stores would have failed the build before it could be reviewed. The extraction is its own change, and the lint allowlist does not move in it. Five components, each cut on a boundary the migration already needs: the AI provider picker, the CLI status list, the API key field, the job-search geo target and the danger zone. The section wrapper stays on the page, which keeps every child host somewhere nothing lays it out - the trap that cost Profile its paired-field row - and the primitives the page and the children both need live in one partial that names its six consumers in its header, because Angular scopes styles to a template and no template shows that dependency. Two things had to be rebuilt rather than inherited: the gap that used to separate the blocks now inside a single child, **measured at 16px on screen against the 16px it replaced**, and the CLI status list's position between the provider picker and the model row, which a sibling component cannot occupy - the picker projects it. Dead CSS removed: three `.about` rules that had reached nothing since that block became its own component. **Checked on a rendered screen in both themes**, including all three CLI states, all three geo modes and the reset confirmation. Template 580 -> 214, stylesheet 362 -> 71. The screen had no tests at all; it has 30.

- **The CV library migrates, in three stores rather than two.** Allowlist 4 -> 3. `cv-list` is the cover-letter library's shape with importing bolted on, and the three concerns each got their own store: `CvListStore` (the documents, the templates, the two job lists, and the in-flight export or delete), `CvImportStore` (upload, hash, parse, preview, save) and `CvGenerateStore` (the baseline generator). **The two-store reading was rejected on measurement, not taste**: folding import and generate together lands at 230-260 lines against a 250 budget, and this campaign has already underestimated a store's size twice. The three came in at 150, 152 and 189, and the component fell **386 -> 180**. **The screen loads once** - the other two stores take the templates and job lists as arguments, because two stores fetching their own can disagree after a change and nothing on screen would say which is stale. **Generating against a tracked job writes the link in the same call**, rather than leaving the page a second step: a forgotten link produces a CV that looks exactly like a linked one, so the mistake is invisible. **Re-picking a file you already imported costs nothing** - the store recognises it from the hash and opens the existing document before the model is called. `suggestCvFilename` moved next to its cover-letter twin with the six tests it never had, taking `cv-content.util.ts` 621 -> 596 against its 400 budget. One dead signal deleted: `generateStep` was written twice and read nowhere, including the template. **Checked on a rendered screen**, list and both modals, including the AI and import paths under a temporary console-only Tauri stub removed in the same call.

- **The cover-letter library migrates, and two stores in one folder now answer differently on purpose.** Allowlist 5 -> 4. Same two-feature shape as My Jobs: `CoverLetterListStore` owns the documents, the lists needed to name which job each belongs to, and the in-flight export or delete; `CoverLetterGenerateStore` owns the modal and the AI call. **The inconsistency is deliberate**: the older `CoverLetterAiStore` in the same folder throws `CoverLetterNoProfileError`, while the new store returns `'busy' | 'no-profile' | 'bad-json' | 'generated' | 'failed'` and never rejects - a missing profile is a refusal rather than a failure, because the user simply has not filled the profile yet. Converting the neighbour was refused as a second migration riding inside this one. Three things were reused rather than re-decided: the codec is passed in because `cleanJsonText` lives in the app, the skill name and generic job-description placeholder come from the layer's existing generation module, and the save dialog stays on the page. **All three labels stayed with the page** - the copy's name, the generated document's name and the linked-job line - because each needs translations or a separator; the stores publish facts. **One named behaviour change**: a label made entirely of punctuation used to suggest a file called `.pdf`, hidden and nameless on most systems; it falls back to `cover-letter` now. **Checked on a rendered screen**, including the AI path under a temporary console-only Tauri stub that was removed in the same call: all four outcomes exercised against the real code path, and without the stub a failed profile _read_ correctly reports `failed` rather than `no-profile` - the distinction the outcome type exists for. The component fell 283 -> 127 lines.

- **The interview stage detail screen migrates, and a refusal that kept someone else's error is fixed.** Allowlist 6 -> 5. This was the densest entry left - eight gateway calls in 332 lines across load, create, update, delete and a two-write reorder. `InterviewStagesStore` owns the timeline, the add/edit modal and its form, the status menu and the delete confirmation; `interview-stage-form.ts` beside it takes the form shape and the mapping that turns blank strings into **absent** fields rather than empty ones - a distinction that is not cosmetic, since an absent optional stays null in the row while `''` is a value. Extracting that module is also what took the store from 256 to 217 against its 250 budget, rather than asking for an exception; it was the correct move on its own terms, which is the only reason it was not a dodge. **Five refusals now say nothing** - a blank label, an unchanged status, a reorder past either end, a save already running, a delete with nothing targeted - while only a real failure speaks. **The defect**: those refusal paths returned _before_ clearing `error`, so a refusal after a failed load left the earlier message standing. Nothing displayed it, and no gate, test or screenshot could have caught it; it was visible only because the browser had just failed a load for real and the next thing tried was a refusal. "Refusal leaves `error` empty" has to mean _clears it_, not _does not set it_. **The reorder keeps its pessimism**: nothing moves on screen until both writes return, so a failed swap leaves the timeline as it was - the opposite choice from the Kanban board, which moves first and rolls back, and both are right for their surface. `fmtDate` stays on the page with its hardcoded `en-GB`, one of the five filed sites. The component fell 332 -> 132 lines.

- **Profile migrates as three stores, and the third one exists because a number said so.** Allowlist 7 -> 6. This is the page ADR-0005 stopped at 445/400 by decision. The grilling gate chose two stores on my estimate that `ProfileStore` would land at 200-220 lines; it landed at **324 against a 250 budget** and the file-size gate refused it, so the decision went back to the maintainer with the real number. `ProfileFormStore` is that correction, and the seam it exposed is a good one that was not visible from outside: **what is saved** and **what is being typed** are different things. The dependency runs one way - `ProfileStore` reads the editor to decide whether the page is dirty; the editor knows nothing about the row. **One writer survived the split on purpose**: `persist` maintains `savedMdHash` alongside the row, and the comment on it records that every writer maintaining the hash by hand eventually forgot to - so `ProfileArtifactStore` computes its patch and hands it over rather than writing itself. **No status sentence moved.** `saveStatus`, `scoreStatus` and `pitchStatus` are translated text built from a date, token counts or an error; the stores publish outcomes - `lastSavedAt`, `error`, `tokens`, and a four-value artefact result where two values are refusals that never toast - and the page writes every word. **A real defect was found on the rendered screen and fixed**: `generate` documented that it never rejects, but `hashText` was called outside the guard, so in the browser a failed hash rejected out of the click handler and nothing appeared at all. It reports `failed` like any other failure now, with a test. Checked on a rendered screen, including the distinction the whole freshness rule rests on: editing a field moves scoring to `unsaved`, while editing only an archetype leaves the page dirty with the artefacts still `fresh`. The component fell 483 -> 171 lines.

- **My Jobs migrates as two stores, because it was two features.** Allowlist 8 -> 7. A sortable table over the local job overview, and a tracklist import wizard with eight signals, an AI call and a three-step flow, had been sharing one class. `MyJobsStore` takes the search, the three filters, the sort pair, the show-analysed toggle, the derived view and the delete confirmation; `TracklistImportStore` takes the flow and its counts. One store would have opened at the 250 budget and made the read-only table depend on `AiService`. **The rows stayed in `libs/data`**: `JobsStore` is the shared cache two screens read, and a copy in the application layer could disagree with it - so this is the first store here that depends on another store rather than on the gateway directly. **Moving the state forced a real distinction into the open**: the empty state asked "are there no jobs", which is not the same question as "did the filters hide them", and a user with 200 jobs and a typo in the search box must not be told they have none - that is now `isEmpty`, separate from an empty `view`.
