It is a **descendant** selector, and Angular's emulated encapsulation stamps the page's `_ngcontent` attribute on the selector's subject - so a trigger button rendered by a child would keep `.dv-btn`'s default sizing and silently break the toolbar. `.dv-linkbtn` is the same problem for the Clear action inside each menu's foot: three of its five uses are exactly the ones that move. `.dv-geomenu__hint` is a third: the page renders it in each panel's empty state and the shell will render it in the Locations foot, so it can be scoped to neither. All three are in `_discover-controls.scss` now, emitted once from `styles.scss` beside `.dv-btn`, `.dv-iconbtn` and the badges. `.dv-filters .dv-btn` at (0,2,0) still beats `.dv-btn` at (0,1,0) regardless of injection order, so the specificity check that mattered for `.dv-arch-badge` applies here too and passes for the same reason. Both names are Discover-only across every template in `apps` and `libs`, so nothing else can start matching them. Stylesheet **806 -> 748**, partial 274 -> 316. Nothing renders differently; `quality:style-move` reports the move lossless.

- **Discover's feed row is its own component, and the `.dv-row` family turns out not to need a hoist at all.** The previous handoff read `.dv-row` as the hard case - shared between the feed and the detail body, so needing the hoist-then-cut the hero got. It is not: the classes the two screens genuinely share went into `_discover-controls.scss` in the earlier hoist, and what was left splits into two **disjoint** sets. Feed chrome - the row, its main hit area, the title, company, meta and action columns - is rendered only by the feed; the detail-body typography (`__detailloading`, `__detail`, `__dh`, `__dp`, `__dul`) only inside `.dv-detail__main`. So the feed row moved straight out. Five values in and three actions out: the row itself, plus the source label, the age, the archetype badge and the matched keywords, all four already resolved on the page because **the detail hero takes the same four** - `heroArchetype` is `rowArchetype` now, and its `HeroArchetype` type is `RowArchetype` beside `FeedRow`, since neither component owns the other. Template **636 -> 567**, stylesheet **903 -> 806**, class **884 -> 878**. **The separator between rows moved to the child's `:host`**, and that is the whole risk of this cut: the page's `.dv-feed > :last-child { border-bottom: none }` now matches the component host, so a border left on `.dv-row` inside it would have survived that rule and drawn a line under the last row - the same host-element shape as the `flex: 1` regression in Profile, and equally invisible to every gate. It is the one declaration `quality:style-move` reports as moved rather than preserved; everything else in the split is lossless. Four page icons the row took with it are gone, which is what paid for the new import and kept the class under its ratchet. All five mutations die, including the two worth naming: the tier key rendered as the badge label, which tints correctly and reads "primary", and Save and Dismiss emitting each other's output.

- **Discover's detail hero is its own component, and the detail screen is now a shell.** The back link, the company mark, the source and age line, the match chip, the title, the archetype badge, the company and location row, the matched keywords and Save. Everything it shows is resolved on the page, because the feed needs the same answers for every row it lists - `srcLabel`, `ago`, `archetypeBadge` and `matchedKeywords` are shared with it, and `detailScore` and `detailVerdict` with the sidebar - so seven values go in and two actions come out. `initials` came along, being rendered nowhere else. With the sidebar gone in the previous cut, `.dv-detail` now holds only the grid, main, loading and actions shells. Stylesheet **1091 -> 938**, template **693 -> 636**; across the three Discover PRs the largest file in the project went **1464 -> 938**. The `.dv-detail__heromain .dv-arch-badge` override travelled with the markup, as the hoist that preceded it predicted. **The class did not shrink**: removing `initials` paid for the new `heroArchetype` helper and the import, so it holds at 884 - the ratchet refused the first attempt at 887 and the fix was to let the helper type its own return rather than import an interface, not to trim anything. Two of six mutations survived: a match chip pinned to one tier, which reads correctly on that tier and mis-tints every other score, and `heroArchetype` returning the tier key as its own label, which the new page helper had no test for at all.

- **Discover's shared row vocabulary is hoisted, ahead of the cut that needs it.** The detail screen renders the "new" marker, the saved badge, the matched-keyword strip and the archetype badge beside the feed that owns them, and all four were defined in `discover.component.scss`. A component's compiled styles never reach another component's template, so the moment any part of the detail screen becomes a child those four render unstyled - the same failure that made `_profile-shell.scss` necessary. They are in `_discover-controls.scss` now, emitted once from `styles.scss`. **No page-root wrapper**, unlike Profile: 33 of Discover's 34 top-level selectors already carry the `dv-` prefix, which is the case `CODE_QUALITY.md` says prefixing is preferable for. Stylesheet **1169 -> 1091**. One override was checked rather than assumed: `.dv-detail__heromain .dv-arch-badge` stays on the page while its base rule leaves, and at specificity (0,2,0) against (0,1,0) it still wins regardless of style-injection order - the co-movement rule bites on same-specificity modifiers, not on contextual descendants. Nothing renders differently; `quality:style-move` reports the move lossless.

- **Discover's detail sidebar is its own component, and the largest file in the project takes its first cut.** `discover.component.scss` was 1464 lines against a 400 budget - the biggest file Applye has - and the only part of Discover never touched, its template and class having been reduced once already. The seam is the job-detail screen's `<aside>`: the raw-score ring, the salary comparison, the job facts and the local-only note. Every class it renders is used nowhere else on the page, and the detail screen is itself mutually exclusive with the rest of Discover - every other block is guarded by `detailId() === null`. The page keeps owning the state, because `detailScore` and `detailVerdict` are also read by the detail hero and the tip line needs the page's matched keywords, so nine values go in and one action comes out. Two pure helpers came with the markup because nothing else named them: the ring's `stroke-dasharray` and the compensation label. Stylesheet **1464 -> 1169**, template **808 -> 693**, class **890 -> 884**; the new component's own stylesheet is 301, within budget. Discover needed **no page-scoped wrapper** - 33 of its 34 top-level selectors already carry the `dv-` prefix, which is the case `CODE_QUALITY.md` says prefixing is preferable for. One of five mutations survived: swapping the "above" and "within" compensation labels left all four distinct, which was all the test checked, while telling a user their salary target was beaten when it was only met.

- **The display-name rules leave the Profile component and get tested directly.** Two rules, inverses of each other, both sitting inline in the page: the load-time backfill that derives first/last from the display name for profiles predating those fields, and the edit-time rule that keeps the display name following the parts until the user takes it over. Both partner `splitDisplayName`, which has always lived in `libs/core`, so having them in a component was the odd part. They are `withBackfilledNameParts` and `withComposedName` in `profile-name.util.ts` now, beside the page like the parse and artefact utils - not in `libs/core`, because nothing outside this page needs them and a `libs/` public API change is a maintainer decision rather than a refactor. Fourteen tests cover the rules directly, where previously only seven exercised them through the component; those seven stay and now prove the wiring rather than the rule. All five mutations die, including the one worth naming: judging "was the name still following the parts" against the _new_ parts instead of the previous ones, which quietly stops the display name updating at all. Class **466 -> 445**.

- **Profile's two AI generators are one method, and the rules that differ are pure functions.** Scoring and the pitch were 117 lines of the same sequence twice - capture the markdown, hash it, short-circuit if cached, render a skill, run it, write the row, report tokens - and they had already drifted: the pitch's comment referred the reader to the scoring copy for the rule both depend on. What actually differs is now in `profile-artifact.util.ts` as three pure functions: how the cache is keyed, what to render in which language, and which columns the write owns. **That last one is a whole-row-replace guard**: `db_upsert_profile` overwrites every column it names, so an artefact write has to carry the other artefact across or generating a pitch wipes the scoring profile. It was correct in both copies and is now stated once, with tests. Scoring stays pinned to English on purpose - it is machine-read when matching a job, and a scoring profile in the user's own language would not compare against job text read in English - while the pitch follows the document language, because the user reads it aloud. Class **496 -> 466**, and 14 new tests cover rules that previously existed only inside two method bodies. **One of six mutations survived the first pass**: addressing the scoring status signals from a pitch run spins the wrong card and puts the token count on the wrong status line, while the row is written correctly and every existing freshness test still passes. Four wiring tests close it.

- **Profile's raw editor is its own component, and the page's template is finally within budget.** The textarea, the scaffold hint and the `profile-import` parse with its reviewable preview. The markdown itself stays on the page - it is `fullMd`, which the save path, the dirty check and both AI calls all read - so it arrives as an input and every keystroke leaves as an output. The parse does not: nothing outside the block reads `parsing`, the status pair or the preview, and the page needs only the result, which `applyParsedProfile` folds into the form and the four section signals before switching back to the Form tab. So the whole pipeline moved and emits once, on apply - **two inputs and two outputs**, against the eleven inputs AI Tools shipped with. `applyParsedProfile` now takes the parsed object as an argument instead of reading a signal, which is what let the signal leave. **The template lands at 270 against a 300 budget** - the third file this campaign has taken from over budget to under, after `cover-letter-detail.component.scss` and `profile.component.scss`. Class **572 -> 496**, stylesheet **211 -> 124**. The parse orchestration had no tests at all (`profile-parse.util.spec.ts` covers the pure mapping, not the call); it has eleven now, and all five mutations die on the first pass - including an array passed off as a profile, which would blank the form.

- **Profile's photo card is its own component, and it takes the whole save path with it.** The first Profile cut that is class-side rather than template-side: the photo is not part of the profile form, never reaches `fullMd`, and has its own backend command precisely because `db_upsert_profile` would otherwise overwrite it. So pick, crop and persist all moved together - routing the write back through the page would have spread one save path across the boundary for nothing. The page keeps only what it kept for the other eight sections, the collapse state, and that was deliberate: the child owning its own `open` would silently have dropped the re-collapse that happens when the user leaves raw mode, which was checked in the running page and still works. `photo` is a seed rather than a binding - a `linkedSignal` off the profile row, so a local edit outlives an unchanged input and a reloaded profile re-seeds it. **Two of five mutations survived the first pass**, and both were real gaps rather than noise: the test that claimed the crop modal closes never opened it, so "it closed" was true before the call; and the rollback test never let the on-screen value diverge from the seed, so restoring the wrong one of the two looked identical. Both are now covered, and all five mutations die. Template **409 -> 342**, class **628 -> 572**, stylesheet **231 -> 211**. `ScoringSummaryComponent` was deleted from the page's `imports:` in the same change - it moved into `profile-ai-tools` and the entry was left behind, which the Angular compiler had been reporting as an unused-import warning.

- **Profile's contact block is one field component rendered nine times.** Name, first name, last name, current role, location, email, phone, website and LinkedIn were nine copies of the same eight lines - a `field` wrapper, a `field__label` bound by id, and a `field__input` bound to one property of the form - differing only in the key, the value, the input type and whether they carried a placeholder. They are now `profile-text-field`, which derives both the element id (`field-first-name`) and the translation key (`profile.field_first_name`) from one `key` input. That pairing is load-bearing rather than tidy: `focusField` scrolls to `field-<key>` when the completeness hero reports a field missing, and there is a test pinning it. The component has no stylesheet, because `field`, `field__label` and `field__input` are already in the page partial. **The file-size ratchet refused this cut**, since a template-only change still adds two lines to an over-budget class, so the parse-preview mapping came out with it as `profile-parse.util.ts`: five pure functions and the tolerant AI-output type, with tests for the rule that had none - blank scalars keep what the form has, structured sections are replaced wholesale. Template **464 -> 409**, class **682 -> 628**.

- **Profile's AI Tools section is its own component, and the freshness chips are defined once.** The scoring card and the pitch card ended with the same eighteen lines - three mutually exclusive chips and a status line - differing only in which hint they showed, so those are now `profile-freshness-chips`, rendered twice. The section around them takes what to show and emits when the user asks: the two AI calls stay on the page, because they read `fullMd`, write the profile row and refresh `savedMdHash`, which is the page's own save path. That is a wider boundary than the five editor sections - eleven inputs rather than two - but they are flat scalars rather than a protocol threaded per field, which is the line `cv-preview` was judged to fall on the wrong side of. Template **603 -> 464**, class **689 -> 682**. One deliberate deletion: `.json-block` shared a grouped selector with `.output-block` and is used nowhere in the repository, so it was dropped rather than carried into a new file - `quality:style-move` reports it, and nothing else.

- **Profile's skills section is its own component, and the page's stylesheet is now within budget.** The last of the five editor sections. It differs from the other four in where its data lives: skills are a plain `string[]` in `form().skills` with no structured mirror, so the section takes that array and emits a new one straight into `updateField`. The chip field carries `id="field-skills"` with it, because that is the anchor the completeness hero scrolls to when skills are missing. `profile.component.scss` finishes at **423 -> 379 against a 400 budget** - the second file this campaign has taken from over budget to under, after `cover-letter-detail.component.scss`. Template **654 -> 603**, class **702 -> 689**. Across the five cuts and the hoist, Profile has gone from **1037 / 772 / 733** to **603 / 689 / 379**.

- **Profile's education section is its own component, and it needs no stylesheet at all.** The fourth section cut, and the one that shows what the shared-style hoist was for: **every class the education markup renders was already in `_profile-shell.scss`**, so the new component ships with a template and a class and no `styleUrl`. Checked in the running page rather than assumed - the card border and radius, the input height and border, the dashed button and the collapse title all compute correctly inside a component that defines none of them. Otherwise the same shape as languages: three list transforms, folded back into `form().education` by the page. Template **741 -> 654**, class **709 -> 702**.

- **Profile's languages section is its own component.** The same shape as work experience and simpler: a list of languages with a CEFR level each, three list transforms, folded back into `form().languages` by the page. The CEFR level list moved into the section, since nothing else offered it. `lang-list` and `lang-row` were the only classes left exclusive after the shared-style hoist. Template **809 -> 741**, class **717 -> 709**, stylesheet **433 -> 423**. Verified in the running page in both directions: adding a language, typing "Ukrainian" and picking "Native" put `Ukrainian (Native)` under `## Languages` in the raw markdown, and leaving raw mode parsed it straight back into the section.

- **Profile's work-experience section is its own component.** The largest of the page's five editor sections, and the first with real logic in it: six list transforms, three of them addressing a bullet inside a position by two indices. Same boundary as target roles - the page owns the entries, the section renders them and emits a new list - with one difference that decided the shape. Every experience edit is also folded back into `form().experienceText`, which is what actually reaches `fullMd`, and that fold uses the page's own form helpers. So it stays on the page: the section emits the whole list and the page sets and syncs once, rather than half the save path living in a child that knows nothing about the form. Only two classes, `exp-bullets` and `exp-bullet-row`, were left exclusive after the shared-style hoist. Template **932 -> 809**, class **751 -> 717**, stylesheet **444 -> 433**. Verified by driving the running page all the way through the fold: adding a position, typing a role and adding a bullet all reach the markdown - the raw view shows `### Staff Engineer` under `## Experience`, and the unsaved-changes hint appears, which it can only do if the fold ran.

- **Profile's target-roles section is its own component.** The first cut the shared-style hoist was meant to unlock, and it was mechanical exactly as promised: the section had been audited and discarded once before, because 23 of the 29 classes its markup names were still used by the rest of the page, and all 23 are now in `_profile-shell.scss`. Five classes were left exclusive to it, and those moved with the markup. The boundary is inputs and outputs, not a service: the page seeds the roles from the profile row, computes `dirty` from them and writes them back on save, so it owns the list and the section only renders it and asks for changes - the same arrangement `cover-letter-block` uses, and the opposite of the onboarding panels, where the child owned state the wizard read back. `isMatchable` disappeared rather than moving, because it was a one-line wrapper the section can call directly. Template **1037 -> 932**, class **772 -> 751**, stylesheet **482 -> 444**. Nothing renders or behaves differently, and this time that was checked by driving the running page: adding a role, typing a generic name to raise the match warning, typing a distinctive one to clear it, and collapsing the section all work through the new boundary.

- **Profile's shared styles are defined once, and the partial is scoped to the page.** Profile is a header, a target-roles card, a photo card, a five-section editor and an AI-tools card in one 733-line stylesheet, and its five editor sections are the next cuts. Measured from the template: of the 95 classes it uses, 30 appear in more than one block. Hoisting them was supposed to be mechanical, and it was not - unlike `ob__` and `dv-`, **Profile's vocabulary is unprefixed and generic**, and seven of those names (`eyebrow`, `muted`, `status`, `status--error`, `section`, `btn-ghost`, `field`) are already defined, with different values, by eight other stylesheets including Settings, Dashboard and Jobs. An unwrapped global partial would have handed those pages every property they do not set themselves - Settings' eyebrow would have picked up a `font-weight` it never asked for - and nothing in the gates catches that. So `_profile-shell.scss` nests everything it owns under `.profile`: no other page can match it, and a child extracted out of Profile still renders inside `.profile`, so it still inherits. `.status--warn` was hoisted alongside `.status` because a base and its modifier that both set `color` are decided by source order while they share a file and by style-injection order once they do not. The page's stylesheet goes **733 -> 482** lines with no other change, and the five section cuts become mechanical. Verified in the running app: every hoisted class computes the same values it did, and all 36 rules are `.profile`-scoped, which no other page has an ancestor for.

- **The losslessness check can now see a hoist into a page-scoped partial.** Every rule nested under a page root gains an ancestor, so `quality:style-move` reported Profile's whole vocabulary as lost and an identical set as gained - the check that exists to catch a real loss could not read the move at all. `--page-scope '.profile'` strips exactly one leading ancestor from the after side before comparing. It is an assertion, not an escape hatch: it removes one ancestor and nothing else, so a declaration genuinely dropped inside the wrapper is still reported, and that case has its own test.

- **The onboarding wizard's Review step is its own component.** The step that shows what was parsed out of a resume and lets the user correct it was 115 template lines and about 60 lines of class. Its stylesheet side was the cleanest seam the wizard has offered: the warning card, the two-column grid, the experience list and the skill chips are used by no other step and by nothing else in the app, and everything else the step renders - the heading set, the field and input primitives, the section and muted-note pieces - was already in `_onboarding-shell.scss` from the earlier hoist, so nothing new had to be shared. The class side is a service rather than inputs, for the same reason the two AI panels are: the wizard reads the five contact fields back for the Ready summary, the profile it saves and the CV document it writes, so passing them down and eventing them back up would have split that save flow across the boundary. `OnboardingReviewService` now owns the parsed CV, the five editable fields, the name-confirm rule and the seeding, and `onboarding-review-step` renders them. Template **628 -> 514**, class **797 -> 738**, stylesheet **718 -> 642**; all three were over budget before and remain so. Nothing renders or behaves differently.

- **The onboarding wizard no longer contains either AI panel.** The CLI-bridge panel followed the API-key one out: `OnboardingCliBridgeService` owns the CLI probe results, the install in flight and the setup card, and `onboarding-cli-card` renders them. The wizard's AI step is now the provider grid and an `@if` choosing between two components. The provider list, the npm package table and the card-title rule became pure functions rather than a third service - the title rule takes the mode as a parameter, because the panel already knows the answer and the provider grid above it does not. Template **742 -> 628**, class **897 -> 797**, stylesheet **766 -> 718**; across both cuts the wizard is down from 878/1002/1045. Nothing renders or behaves differently.

- **The onboarding wizard's API-key panel is its own component.** The AI step renders one of two panels - the CLI bridge or the API key form - and the key panel was 137 template lines naming 15 members the rest of the wizard's markup never touches. Seven of those are pure internals, but seven are state the wizard reads back: whether a key is stored gates the Continue button and the Ready step's summary, and the quality/economy model pair is what gets written into settings. So the state moved into a service the wizard provides and the card mutates, rather than being passed down and evented back up, which would have split the save flow across the boundary. That is the arrangement the Discover Sources drawer already uses. Eight more classes - the panel shell and the numbered step list, shared by both panels - joined the wizard's global partial first. Template **878 -> 742**, class **1002 -> 897**, stylesheet **894 -> 766**; all three were over budget before and remain so. Nothing renders or behaves differently.

- **The cover-letter editor's five text blocks are one component.** Date, subject, greeting, closing and signature each rendered a collapsible section around one text field. Normalising the block key out of the markup showed subject, greeting, closing and signature to be **identical, line for line**, and date to be the same thing without a Regenerate button - 308 template lines that were one component written five times. They are now `cover-letter-block`, rendered five times, and the proof it is faithful runs the other way: substituting each key back into the component reproduces all five original blocks exactly. The three action classes the page still needs for its recipient and body blocks moved to a partial emitted once from `styles.scss`, and the word-count rules became pure functions with their own tests. Template **911 -> 669**, class **653 -> 644**, stylesheet **410 -> 332** - the stylesheet is now **within budget**. Nothing renders or behaves differently.

- **The Sources drawer is its own component, and the sources list its own service.** Discover is the largest page in the project by some distance - its stylesheet alone was 1915 lines against a 400 budget - and it had never been cut. A consumer audit found the drawer the cleanest seam available: of the twenty-five symbols its markup names, **twenty-three are used nowhere else on the page**. The list itself moves to `DiscoverSourcesService`, which the page provides, because both sides genuinely read it - the page scans over the enabled sources and counts them, the drawer adds, removes and switches them. Passing it down and eventing back up would have stranded the drawer's optimistic toggle, which flips a row before the write lands so the checkbox does not lag the click, on the wrong side of the boundary. Stylesheet **1915 -> 1466**, template **1070 -> 808**, class **1069 -> 890**; all three are still over budget. No behaviour changed.

- **Discover's button, icon button, text input and overlay are defined once.** Extracting the drawer would have duplicated them, because style encapsulation stops the page's stylesheet at a child component's boundary. They now sit in a partial emitted globally from `styles.scss`, which is the same decision `_editor-shell.scss` already records for the document editors, and which is what keeps the new stylesheet inside its budget rather than shipping the same rules twice.

- **The onboarding wizard's shared styles are defined once.** The wizard is six step blocks in one 1045-line stylesheet, and the steps are being extracted into their own components. Angular scopes a component's compiled CSS, so a class defined on the page does not reach a child extracted out of it - which means every one of those five later cuts would have had to decide again what to do with the same shared classes, and the last cut that made that decision under time pressure lost a rule. Measured from the template instead: of the 130 classes it uses, exactly 14 appear in more than one step - the heading set, the card and badge primitives, the form primitives, and the spinner and key-status pieces the AI-setup and resume steps share. Those move into `_onboarding-shell.scss`, emitted once from `styles.scss`, the same arrangement `_editor-shell.scss` already uses for the document editors. The page's stylesheet goes **1045 -> 894** lines with no other change, and the remaining five cuts become mechanical. Nothing renders differently: the page's stylesheet no longer mentions a single hoisted class, so no specificity or ordering interaction is possible, and `ob__` is used nowhere else in the app. The initial bundle is 0.5 kB smaller than on `main`, because the rules are no longer scoped per component.

- **Exported documents are named in words, not in slug.** The save dialog offered `jetbrains_-_senior_software_developer_-_tailored_cv.pdf` for a document labelled "JetBrains - Senior Software Developer - Tailored CV": the old rule lower-cased everything, kept hyphens and turned spaces into underscores, so every separator came out as the unreadable `_-_`. The same document is now offered as **`JetBrains Senior Software Developer Tailored CV.pdf`** - the label's own words, single spaces between them, no hyphens and no underscores. Case is kept, because "JetBrains" is a name and "jetbrains" is not, and letters outside ASCII survive for the same reason: a company called Zürich no longer exports as `z_rich`. Characters a filesystem refuses are dropped rather than replaced, so removing one does not widen the gap between two words, and a name can no longer begin or end with a dot or a space - legal to write on macOS and Linux, silently trimmed by Windows. This is only the suggested name; the save dialog still lets you type anything.

### Fixed

- **Saving an edited CV no longer fails with `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed`.** `document_library.theme_id` is a real foreign key into `cv_themes`, and the CV editor always writes a theme - its picker defaults to Classic, id 1. On a database whose `cv_themes` row is missing, that made **every** CV save fail, permanently, with a raw SQLite error (`code: 787`) the user could do nothing about. `cv_templates` and `template_id` have the same shape. Both tables are pure lookup mirrors of constants that also live in `libs/core`, so a migration now puts the built-in rows back where they are absent; it is a no-op on a database that already has them. What emptied them in the first place is not established and is worth watching - this makes the app able to recover instead of bricking its own save path. Found on a real install whose `cv_themes` and `cv_templates` were both empty while both seed migrations were recorded as applied, with checksums matching their files byte for byte.

- **The Rust tests can now see a foreign-key violation at all.** `test_pool` never turned foreign keys on, and SQLite defaults them **off**, while the real pool is built with `.foreign_keys(true)`. Every constraint in the schema was therefore unenforced in tests - which is why the save bug above reached a user with the suite green. The new regression test builds its pool the way the app does.
- **"Open file" and "Show in folder" work on an exported document again.** Both commands hand a path to the OS launcher, so they are guarded - the point being that a bug or a compromised renderer must not be able to ask the backend to launch an arbitrary file. The guard admitted only files under the app's own data directory, which was true of the journal exports it was written for and is not true of the apply wizard's Export PDF: that writes wherever the save dialog pointed, usually Downloads. Applye therefore refused to open the file it had just written, with `refused: that file is outside Applye's own document folder` printed directly beneath the path it had just reported saving. A path now qualifies either by sitting under the data directory **or** by having been written by Applye during this run - provenance rather than location, so the threat model is kept rather than traded away. Only the extensions Applye actually exports (`pdf`, `docx`) are ever remembered, which keeps the launcher pointed at documents and never at something the OS would execute; the record is in memory only, so it is what this run wrote, not a growing list of everywhere the user has saved.
- **The job page's icon guardrail had been quietly narrowing with every split.** It reads the template, collects every `icons.*` reference and asserts the table defines them - but it only ever read `jobs.component.html`, and each component extracted from that page took its icon references somewhere the guard could not see. After this split the page was down to nine references and the guard's own "did the regex match anything" assertion fired, which is the only reason it surfaced. The templates are now discovered by asking which components read the table, so a child's `icons.doesNotExist` is caught in the fast gate as it always was for the page, and future splits stay covered without anyone remembering to come back. Coverage went from 9 icon references to 26.

### Changed

- **The wizard's export-and-apply step is its own component.** The jobs page is over budget in its template, its class and its stylesheet at once, and this step was 107 template lines of it. Everything it renders comes from services the page already provides component-scoped, so the new component injects them directly instead of taking them as inputs - which retires the four `DocumentExportService` aliases and the three one-line forwarding methods the page kept only so its template could name them. `Start over` stays on the page and is emitted, because it resets the tailoring, the score and the export state and then moves the wizard back to step 1. Template **786 -> 686**, class **1068 -> 1050**, stylesheet **624 -> 493**; all three are still over budget. No behaviour changed.

### Fixed

- **"Generating" no longer sticks forever on the CV card after you leave the job page and come back.** The CV draft asks two questions of the user through the gap dialog - once for what the job wants and the profile lacks, and again for any entry the model could not date. The dialog's service is scoped to the job page, so leaving the page destroys it while the generation carries on in the background, exactly as intended. The second ask then landed on that dead instance: it set "open" on signals no template was reading any more, so no dialog ever appeared, nobody could answer it, and the promise behind it never settled. The card sat on **Generating** with nothing on screen and nothing happening, and no error, because nothing had failed - the run was simply waiting for an answer it could never receive. A retired dialog now answers `null` instead of opening, which is what a cancel already does; gap-fill is optional on every path, so the CV is still produced. The cover letter was unaffected because it skips gap-fill entirely while a CV is generating.

- **Dependency-bump pull requests can pass CI again.** The attribution gate landed on 2026-07-31 and rejects any `Signed-off-by` trailer, but Dependabot signs every commit it opens, so every automated bump has failed the gate since - starting with the `ip-address` bump, whose lint, tests, build and file-size checks were all green. The gate exists to keep human and agent attribution off commits written by the maintainer; a bot signing its own dependency bump is not that. Commits are now skipped only when the author name carries the `[bot]` suffix a human GitHub account cannot have **and** the address is the matching GitHub noreply or support address. Either one alone is not enough, so setting a bot name locally does not buy an exemption, and the pull-request body is still inspected in every case.

### Fixed

- **Mark as Applied no longer leaves a second application row behind.** The action wrote its own `saved` row before committing the documents, and that row never reached the signal the job page holds, so the first commit step to ask for a draft found none and wrote a second row for the same job. The status then landed on one of the two and the other was orphaned. Creation now goes through the page's draft hook, which is the single owner of that row and of the signal. Confirmed in the running app: a job marked applied straight from its analysed state now ends up with exactly one row where it previously had two.

- **Mark as Applied says what it is doing.** Committing the documents generates whatever the application is missing, which runs against the configured AI provider and can take minutes. The button simply went dead for the whole time, with no spinner, no text and no toast, which reads as a hang. It now reads "Preparing documents…" while that step runs. `busy` alone could not say it, because saving a lead raises the same flag for an operation that takes a moment.

### Fixed

- **The rule that keeps discover off plaintext HTTP is now tested.** `require_https` is the only thing standing between a source row edited to `http://` and a request sent in the clear, and every fetch in the discover group passes through it - but nothing asserted it. Removing the check entirely left all 339 tests green. It now has a test covering plain HTTP, a missing scheme, an uppercase `HTTPS://` that does not match the check, and the `file:` and `data:` schemes a hand-edited row could otherwise smuggle in. No behaviour changed; the guard simply cannot be weakened silently any more.

### Fixed

- **An AI response that is valid JSON but not a CV no longer becomes an empty draft.** `parseCvSkillResponse` promised to throw on anything it could not read, so the caller could show what actually came back - but it only checked that `JSON.parse` succeeded. A model answering `"I could not read this CV"` in the JSON the prompt asked for parses fine as a string, and every field then fell back to `null`: the user got a blank editor with no reason for it, indistinguishable from a CV the model found nothing in. Numbers, `null` and bare strings are now treated as unparsed, which sends them through the repair pass and then to the error the function always documented. An empty object is still accepted, because a CV with no recognised fields is a real answer. Found by mutation testing during the split below: deleting the throw entirely left all 1195 tests green.

### Changed

- **The apply wizard's tailor step is its own component.** The three phase cards, the base-CV picker that starts a run, the in-flight line with its cancel, and the change and gap notes a finished run produced all come out of the jobs page, which is over budget in its template, its class and its stylesheet at once. The step injects `TailoringService` - which the page already provides component-scoped - rather than taking its state as inputs, which is what retires the seven declarations the page kept only so its template could name them. It deliberately starts nothing: tailoring, cancelling and re-tailoring each continue into page state the step knows nothing about (the export status, the post-tailor rescore, the linked documents), so the step emits and the page decides. Two things the split made testable rather than only moved: an empty base-CV select now provably reads as "tailor from the profile" rather than the document id 0 a bare `+''` produces, and the in-flight check is provably keyed by this job, so another job's run cannot animate this one. Nothing changes on screen. **Template 941 -> 786, class 1080 -> 1068, stylesheet 860 -> 624**, all three still over budget.

- **Reading an AI skill response is its own module.** `cv-content.util.ts` was 829 lines against a 400 budget and held the CV content model next to the code that reads what a model returned - two things that change on different occasions: one when a provider starts wrapping its answer a new way, the other when the CV gains a field. Fence stripping, the truncated-JSON repair, the skill and gap-response parsers and the date-answer parser move to `cv-parse.util.ts`. Their tests were already in a file called `cv-parse.util.spec.ts`, which had been testing a module that did not exist yet. **829 -> 622**, still over budget; the content model is the next cut. Consumers are unaffected: the new module is re-exported from `cv-content.util.ts` for the same reason `cv-style.util` and `cv-entry.util` are, since three of the importing files are themselves over budget and the size gate refuses to let them grow by an import line.

- **Discover's location rules separate from the vocabulary they match against, and three unenforced table rules became tests.** `discover-location.ts` was 910 lines against a 400 budget, of which about 780 were one list: every country, its names and ISO2 code, the cities worth resolving by name, and the US states and Canadian provinces. That list moves to `discover-location-tables.ts` and what stays is the matching itself - whole-word names, codes only as a standalone segment or an uppercase token, cities first, then states, then countries. **910 -> 128.** Three rules the table stated in comments and nothing checked now have tests: the region-generic entries ("Europe", "Asia") must stay last or they swallow the real country that follows, no city token may belong to two countries, and no ISO2 code may either - in each case the earlier entry wins silently and no caller can tell. The two documented collisions are asserted as behaviour rather than prose: `CA` resolves to California, not Canada, and `NL` to the Netherlands, not Newfoundland.

- **The file-size gate stops measuring one flat data table, and says exactly when that is allowed.** A vocabulary file is excluded only when it is a table and nothing else - no branching, no functions, every rule that reads it in another module - because its length then describes how many places a job board can name, not how it is built. The Discover location vocabulary joins the translation catalogue on that list, by exact path: `*-tables.ts` is not a category anyone can opt into. The exclusion has its own test asserting the opposite cases still fail - the rules module beside it, the table's own spec file, and a same-named file anywhere else - because an exclusion that leaks stops measuring the code it exists to keep measurable.

- **Discover's geography splits into scopes and countries, and a silent way to widen a market scan is now tested.** `discover_geo.rs` was 522 lines of source against the 500 budget, holding two things that grow on different occasions: the region scopes, a short list of continent buckets, and the per-country vocabulary - every city, state and spelling a two-letter code also answers to - which grows whenever a market is added or a board starts naming places a new way. The long half moves to `discover_geo_countries.rs`. While splitting, a real coverage gap surfaced: removing `gb` from `KNOWN_COUNTRY_CODES` left all 344 tests green, yet it changes what a scan returns. The "somewhere else" set skips a whole region whose list overlaps the selected market, so for a Germany market the European names come only from each country's own entry - without `gb`, "Remote - London" stops reading as somewhere else and passes a Germany scan on the word "Remote". That list now has a test, as does the deliberate `ca` collision, where Canada gives up its own bare ISO code because in a job location it means California far more often. **522 -> 246**, with 293 in the new module.

- **The from-link flow stopped owning the helpers four other modules were importing from it.** `job_url.rs` was 580 lines of source against the 500 budget, and the excess was code that has nothing to do with classifying a pasted link: turning markup into plain text, reading a value out of an XML tag, and pulling the host, the path segments and a display name out of a URL. Four discover modules already imported those from `job_url`, which is the wrong module to name for a helper that knows nothing about the paste flow. They now live where consumers can name them honestly - `web_text.rs` for `strip_html` and `xml_tag`, `url_parts.rs` for `extract_host`, `path_segments` and `titleize_slug` - with the markup tests moving alongside the code they cover. What stays in `job_url.rs` is the host allowlist and the six per-board readers. `extract_host` gained the tests it never had, which matters because it produces the exact string the allowlist matches a closed board against. **580 -> 399, under budget.**

- **The ATS check's vocabulary is its own module.** `ats.rs` was 619 lines of source against the 500 budget, and most of the excess was one table: the English and German stopword list. That list, the tokenizer that keeps `c++`, `.net`, `node.js` and `ci/cd` intact, and the two rules deciding whether a term looks technical rather than prosaic all move to `ats_tokens.rs`. What stays is the scoring. `ats_format.rs` had been importing `tokenize` back out of `ats.rs`; it now names the module that defines it. **619 -> 289, under budget.**

- **The CLI bridge stopped answering three questions in one file.** `ai/cli.rs` was 668 lines of source against the 500 budget, holding how to run a coding CLI, whether one is installed, and how to install it. The last two moved out: `cli_probe.rs` resolves each supported CLI and asks it for a version for the Settings screen, and `cli_install.rs` is the only part of the bridge that changes the machine rather than reading it - which is exactly why it deserves a file where its safety rules are the first thing visible. Those rules are unchanged: only the packages in the built-in list are ever installed, the provider name from the frontend is matched against that list rather than interpolated into a command, `npm` is resolved to an absolute path and run without a shell, and stderr is truncated before it reaches the UI. **668 -> 420, under budget.**

- **The feed readers split by source family.** `discover_parsers.rs` was 697 lines of source against the 500 budget - a number only visible once Rust source stopped being counted together with inline tests. Two families came out. Hosted ATS boards (Greenhouse, Lever, Ashby, Personio) publish one company's openings at a slug-scoped endpoint, so the company is known before the request is made and each reader takes it as an argument; they are now `discover_parsers_ats.rs`. No Fluff Jobs is the only source needing two requests - its list endpoint carries no description at all - so its list reader, detail reader and salary formatter move together into `discover_parsers_nofluffjobs.rs`. What stays is the shared vocabulary and the single-request feeds. Each new module keeps its tests in a sibling file, following the pattern the parsers already used. **697 -> 406, under budget.**

- **Rust file-size budgets stopped measuring the wrong thing.** Rust keeps its unit tests in the same file by convention, so the old single 800-line rule covered source and tests at once and said little about either: `tailoring.rs` passed at 699 lines of which only 266 were source, while `discover_parsers.rs` passed on nearly the same score with 694 lines of source and 3 of tests. The checker now finds each `#[cfg(test)]` item and counts it against a separate 600-line test budget, leaving Rust source at 500 - stricter than the 800 it replaces, and no longer a reason to push tests out of the file they belong to. Finding those blocks means scanning Rust well enough that a brace inside a raw-string JSON fixture cannot close a test module early, which is covered by tests of its own.

- **`npm run quality:file-size:all` audits the whole repository.** The gate only measures files changed against a base, so a clean report means "nothing in this change is near budget" and never "the repository is within budget". That was misread once and produced a false all-clear in the project state doc. The new mode lists everything over budget or within 20% of it and always exits zero, because failing on debt that predates the rule would only block unrelated work.

- **The apply wizard's document cards are their own component.** The CV and cover-letter review cards - each one's status badge, its create, regenerate and review buttons, and the choose-an-existing select underneath - come out of the jobs page, which is over budget in its template, its class and its stylesheet at once. The cards inject the services the page already provides component-scoped rather than taking them as inputs, which is what retires nine declarations the page was keeping purely so its template could name them. They deliberately do no work: creating a draft, opening the editor and linking a chosen document each continue into flows the cards know nothing about, so those stay on the page. Nothing changes on screen. **Template 1122 -> 941, class 1104 -> 1080, stylesheet 919 -> 860**, all three still over budget.

- **The live discover fetch checks sit with the code they exercise, and a confusing name collision is explained.** The two manually-run network tests build a source row and call the fetch layer, all of which moved out of `discover.rs` earlier; the tests followed. Separately, `archetypes::derive_title_keywords` and `discover_filter::derive_title_keywords` share a name without being the same function - the filter one keeps `+` and `#` as word characters so `c++` and `c#` survive as keywords, the archetype one splits on them and drops anything with a digit. Merging them would change what a scan matches on, so each now says so in its own doc comment rather than leaving the next reader to work it out. **599 -> 537.**

- **Every Rust file is under its size budget, and the discover source registry is its own module.** Which feeds exist and which of them are on is a different question from running a scan, and it is the only part of the discover group the user edits directly: the source list, the enable toggle, the market plan that proposes which built-in sources to switch on, and adding or removing a source. It moves out whole, with its three tests, which borrow the database fixture from the module they left rather than growing a second copy of it. **876 -> 599, under 800 for the first time** - from 1679 at the start, across four modules that each answer one question. This is also the first split in the campaign to move Tauri commands, so the six affected entries in the command registry were repointed; the command names the frontend calls are unchanged. **With this, no Rust file in the repository is over its size budget.**

- **The network half of a scan is its own module, and the parsers stop importing out of it.** `discover.rs` still held the HTTPS layer - the shared client, `require_https`, the JSON and text getters, the Bundesagentur key and its page and detail caps, the two detail fetchers, and the per-source-type dispatch - alongside the database work that consumes it. That layer moves to `discover_fetch.rs`, and `SourceRow` goes with it as the input it reads. `RawJob` and `json_str` moved the other way, into `discover_parsers.rs`: every one of the eleven readers builds `RawJob`s and `json_str` is called sixty times there against once elsewhere, so the parsers had been importing their own vocabulary back out of `discover.rs`. Both directions of that inversion are undone. **1139 -> 876.**

- **The local discover filters are their own module.** `discover.rs` was 1679 lines against an 800 budget and mixed the network half of a scan with the rule that decides whether a fetched job is ever seen. The 0-token half came out whole: the title keyword lists and their archetype fallback, the region and market geo configs, and `geo_passes` with the ordering that makes "somewhere else" beat the word "Remote" - the rule that keeps a "Remote - US only" posting out of a Ukraine market. It is the code that silently drops jobs, so it moves with all 26 of its tests, and it now has a file where nothing around it touches the network or the database. **1679 -> 1139.**

- **The tailoring group is under its size budget, and page geometry is its own module.** Millimetres and margins were the last thing in `tailoring.rs` that was not block styling: nothing in the page resolver reads a font, a colour or a block, and it is deliberately forgiving so a malformed `style_json` degrades to A4 with clamped margins instead of breaking the export. **814 -> 699** - from 2538 at the start, across seven modules that each answer one question.

- **The markdown reader is its own module.** Turning tailored markdown into an untagged block list is the journal export path's front door, and it has nothing to do with resolving an already-built block list against a style. It moves out with the inline `**bold**` splitter both renderers call, and with its four tests. **956 -> 814.**

- **The look a document resolves to is its own module.** The built-in theme table - the hand-maintained Rust mirror of the TS theme descriptor, with its parity test - and the two style cascades that decide what font, size, weight and colour each block actually gets, came out of `tailoring.rs`. What stays is the block list that reads the answer. **1179 -> 956.**

- **The tailoring journal separates from the model it journals.** What the 3-pass run produced, where it was written and how the user opens it - the cache rows, the `generated_docs` rows, the export commands and the containment check behind Open and Reveal - is everything in the tailoring group that touches SQLite, the filesystem or the shell, and nothing else there touches any of them. It moves out, and `tailoring.rs` is left holding only the block model, the theme and the style cascade both renderers read. **1578 -> 1179.**

- **The DOCX renderer is its own module, like the PDF one already was.** `tailoring.rs` was 2087 lines against an 800 budget and still held both exporters around the block model they share. The Office Open XML half came out: paragraph construction, the page section, and the font-embedding pass that makes a Lato CV render as Lato in Word instead of quietly substituting. The model, the theme and the style resolution stay where they were, because both renderers read them and neither owns them. **2087 -> 1578.**

- **The block tests sit with the blocks, and the heading table with the only caller that reads it.** `section_heading` was defined in `documents.rs` and called from nowhere but `documents_blocks.rs`, which imported it back across the module line, and the four tests for `cv_content_to_blocks` were still in `documents.rs` asserting a function defined elsewhere. Both directions of that inversion are undone: the table and the tests moved to the module they describe, and the import disappeared with them. **788 -> 669.**

- **The documents library is under its size budget, and the export path is its own module.** Turning a stored row into DOCX or PDF bytes was the last half `documents.rs` held that was not about storing rows: it reads one document, resolves the style the user persisted over the theme they picked, pulls the photo out of the content, and hands the result to the renderers. It moves whole, with the two tests that cover it, which borrow the database fixture from the module they left rather than growing a second copy of it. **1035 -> 788, under 800 for the first time** - from 1926 at the start of the campaign, across four modules that each answer one question.

- **Reading a CV out of a picked file is its own module.** The DOCX and PDF text readers, the image-magic-byte sniffing and the data-URI codec are what `documents.rs` did before a document exists: no database row, no library item, just bytes the user pointed at. They move out whole, with the panic guard that wraps both third-party parsers, which is the reason this half deserves a file of its own - it is the module where untrusted input arrives. Three doc comments left stranded by the earlier blocks split, describing functions that live in another file now, went with the same pass. **1196 -> 1035.**

- **A document's style is its own module.** `documents.rs` was still 1645 lines against an 800 budget after the blocks split, and the style contract was the part of it that touches neither the database nor the filesystem: the persisted `CvStyle` with its per-section overrides and page geometry, the photo placement, and the two deterministic checks that judge them - the ATS-safety notes and the theme validator. The renderers in `tailoring` already consumed those types across a module line, so they now name the module that owns them instead of the one that stores rows. **1645 -> 1196.**

- **The ATS check's two halves are two modules.** `ats.rs` was 1042 lines against an 800 budget and answered two questions at once: whether the CV says what the posting asked for, and whether a parser can read the CV at all. The second is answered without looking at the posting, so it came out whole - heading recognition, the photo rule per market, and every parsability finding, with the six tests that cover them. The keyword half keeps the shared CV fixture and lends it to the new module rather than copying it, because a CV that is both well-covered and cleanly parseable is the one thing both halves must agree on. **1042 -> 743.**

- **The content-to-blocks conversion is its own module.** `documents.rs` was 1926 lines against an 800 budget; the pure middle of the export path came out - stored CV and cover-letter JSON in, styled blocks out, no database and no filesystem. Both renderers take it from there, so section headings and block tagging can be asserted without producing a file. **1926 -> 1645.**

- **Each user-guide page is its own file, and its own chunk.** `guide-pages.ts` held all eleven at 1122 lines against a 400 budget; each was already a standalone component lazily routed on its own, so splitting them also splits the bundle. Largest file is now 165. All 39 routes still prerender.

- **The PDF renderer is its own module.** `tailoring.rs` was 2538 lines against an 800 budget - the largest Rust file left after the Discover scan engine came apart. The printpdf half moved out: family mapping, glyph measurement, the line wrapper that measures rather than guesses, and the renderer that lays blocks onto pages. It touches no database and no filesystem. The embedded font faces went to a third file, because both exporters need them and leaving them in either would have made the other import its fonts from a module named for a format it does not produce. **2538 -> 2087.**

- **No spec file in the repository is over its budget any more.** The last three - CV detail 940, the live style panel 913, onboarding 689, all against 600 - became seven files. Two needed a shared harness because their setup is knowledge rather than boilerplate: the panel wires a fixture and a change collector, onboarding wires eight mocks whose arrangement is the point, and copying either into a second file would have duplicated the wiring. 1183 tests before and after.

- **The CV content spec follows the modules it tests.** It was 1509 lines against a 600 budget, testing four modules through one file after the source split. Now four files - style, AI response parsing, content building, entry editing - each under budget. 120 tests before and after.

- **Style editing and entry editing left the CV content util.** It was 1245 lines against a 400 budget holding four unrelated jobs; two came out. `cv-style.util.ts` is immutable edits to a style plus the resolution that reads one back, sharing the rule that makes them one thing - the persisted override tree stays minimal, so a style set and unset again is identical to one never touched. `cv-entry.util.ts` changes content where the other changes presentation. **1245 -> 829.**

- **The CV preview's test file is six files, split by behaviour.** It was 2263 lines against a 600 budget - the worst test file in the repository - and the budget rule names the remedy. Core render and geometry, styling, themes, selection, selection identity, and inline leaf editing, each now under budget. The 20-line TestBed setup became a shared harness, which is what made splitting possible: every spec needs every input set, or a test fails on a missing required input instead of on what it was asserting. 133 tests before and after.

- **Discover's tests moved to sit with what they test.** The scan engine's test module was 1142 lines - over the 800 test budget on its own - and still testing three things after the source had been split into three. Twenty-eight reader tests and three geography tests now live beside the code they exercise, so a reader and its fixtures open in one file. `commands/discover.rs` **2096 -> 1679**; every file in the group is now under budget except the scan engine itself.

- **Discover's feed readers are their own module too.** Twelve readers for the shapes the built-in sources actually serve, plus the detail reader for the one source whose list carries no description. None of them touches the network - each takes already-fetched text or JSON and returns a job - which is what makes them testable against a fixture instead of a server. That boundary was already in the code; it just had no file to be a boundary between. `commands/discover.rs` **2742 -> 2096**, with the readers at 666, under budget. Three files where there was one: the scan engine, its geography, its readers.

- **Discover's geography is its own module.** `commands/discover.rs` was 3245 lines against an 800 budget - the largest file in the repository once the site stylesheet was dealt with. The half that came out is pure lookup: freetext names for each region, the tokens a country code also answers to, the US state tables behind them, the market parser and the location matcher. No database, no network, no state; it is the half that changes when a market is added rather than when the scan does. **3245 -> 2742**, with the new module at 522. Gutting the German country tokens turns five tests red, so the moved code is exercised rather than merely compiled.

- **The site stylesheet is split by section, and the compiled CSS is byte-identical.** `apps/web/src/styles.scss` was 2167 lines against a 400 budget - the worst ratio in the repository, and never named in a plan until this week. It already carried its own section banners, so the seams were written in. Eleven partials now, largest 353 lines, and `styles.scss` is the list of `@use` lines - which is not bookkeeping, because Sass emits each module before the file that uses it, in that order, so the list _is_ the cascade. Checked rather than asserted: same md5, and Angular's content hash in the filename is unchanged too. Swapping two partials produces a different hash, so the identical result is evidence rather than coincidence.

- **The Discover scan console has tests, including for what it does when the scan itself dies.** Forty-five of `scan()`'s seventy lines were building console text; they are now functions, and three rules that were only implicit have assertions. A source whose error comes back as an empty string reports as success, because a failure with nothing to say is worse than saying it worked. The result header counts the sources the scan actually reported rather than the ones requested, since one can drop out in between. And when the scan dies, every line still spinning becomes an error, because a spinner claims work that is not happening. `discover.component.ts` **1108 -> 1069**.

- **The Discover feed's filter and its For-you split have tests, and three rules that were only comments are now assertions.** A dismissed row survives every filter, because it renders as the transient Dismissed-Undo strip and a filter that could remove it would take the undo with it. A lone More openings section drops its header so it reads as a plain list, while a lone For you keeps its label, since the label is the only thing saying why those rows come first. And every active filter applies together rather than only the last one. `discover.component.ts` **1163 -> 1108**.

- **The job-description parser behind Discover's detail pane has tests, and two of its sharp edges are now written down.** It recovers structure from plain text - the Rust side emits one line per block tag and drops bullet markers, so bullets, marker-less runs, headings and paragraphs all have to be inferred. Ninety lines of pure heuristics with no test until now. Behaviour is unchanged; where a test disagreed with the code, the test was wrong. Two edges are pinned rather than smoothed: a short prose line ending in a colon reads as a heading, and two consecutive lines of ninety characters or less read as a list. Testing also found a latent bug and left it in place deliberately - the section lexicon holds the stems `responsibilit` and `requirement` followed by a word boundary, so neither ever matches the plural it was written for, and those words are headings only via the colon rule. `discover.component.ts` **1242 -> 1163**.

- **Mark as applied is now beside the other job actions, with tests for two rules it had lost.** It lived on the jobs page while Save and Delete lived in a service, though all three share the same guard, the same status line and the same rule that the overview row mirrors what the database recorded rather than what was asked for. Two invariants that had no test now have one each: the CV and cover letter are committed **before** the status flips, so a job marked applied always owns its documents; and a failed status write leaves the overview row untouched, so no row claims applied for an application that is not. `jobs.component.ts` **1119 -> 1104**.

- **`npm run type-check` now sees Angular templates, which it never did.** It ran `tsc`, and `tsc` does not compile templates: a binding to a member the component does not have, or a type the template cannot accept, passed the fast gate with zero errors and surfaced only under a full build. Four times in one session the build was the only gate to catch one. `ngc` takes the same `--noEmit`, and measured on this repository it is **faster** - 3.48s against 4.31s - as well as strictly more correct, so there was nothing to trade off. Proven on both Angular apps and both failure modes before the switch, and a guardrail test now fails if either app reverts to `tsc`.

- **Three decisions the jobs page made silently now have tests.** Opening a job picks the language the Review documents step works in and the CV the next tailoring builds on; the wizard's three-pass strip decides which pass you are offered. All three were inline in a component with no test, and the CV rule is the one that could cost you: it opens on the profile deliberately, because a CV selected by accident makes the next tailoring build on someone else's document, and it pulls this job's own tailored CV into the list even when the language filter would drop it - without that, a CV in another language vanishes and the choice resets to the profile without saying so. `jobs.component.ts` **1162 -> 1119**.

- **A job you analysed is findable again.** Since PR #248, My Jobs listed only the jobs you claimed - Save this job, or Mark as applied. Everything else was still written to the database, because the score cache, the tailoring and the generated documents all key on `job_id`, and then shown nowhere: paste a description, pay tokens to score it, navigate away, and the result was unreachable with no evidence it existed. Those rows now come back from the query flagged rather than hidden, and My Jobs gains one filter chip, **off by default**, so the table still means the jobs you decided on until you ask otherwise. An unclaimed row shows its own status word - **Analysed**, dashed and muted - rather than an empty cell or a misleading "Saved", and that word joins the status filter like any other. It stays deletable through the trash control that was already there. **No migration:** claimed is derived from whether an application row exists, not stored. A Discover scan writes many rows at once and none of them are your doing, so those stay hidden until claimed, using the rule the other list command already applied. Decided through a grilling rather than chosen, and written up as ADR-0004. What the ADR did not foresee: `listJobsOverview` is shared, so relaxing it silently put unclaimed rows into the dashboard's Recent jobs labelled "Saved" - the exact ambiguity the change exists to remove - and stopped a user with one analysed job from reading as new. The dashboard keeps meaning what it meant, and the rule now lives in a tested pure function. Two new locale keys in all six languages.

- **A failed discard no longer looks exactly like a successful one.** Discarding a tailoring deletes the draft documents it produced. When that delete threw, the catch wrote the error text to the Review documents status line **without** marking it an error and **without** a toast, so it rendered in the ordinary style: a discard that destroyed nothing was indistinguishable from one that worked, and the next thing the user did was decided on that belief. It now reports as an error, and the page resets its own state only when something was actually discarded - a failure leaves the wizard where it was and the confirmation still open, instead of tearing the view down around a tailoring that is still there. The fix needed a seam, so the flow moved to `TailoringDiscardService`, where the failure path can be asserted; which documents a discard may destroy came out as a pure function, and it will never return a document the user committed to their library.

- **The gap-fill bundle has one owner, and the profile write it hides has a test.** The CV and cover letter flows each assembled the same four callbacks inline. The one worth owning centrally is the profile append: `upsertProfile` replaces the whole row, so a payload naming only the text silently discards the scoring cache, the pitch and the archetypes - the bug this repository knows as #97 - and it had no test at all. `jobs.component.ts` **1197 -> 1162**.

- **The Review documents step has one owner for its status line, and the rule that spends tokens has a test.** Nine places on the jobs page repeated the same four moves around that line - clear it, work, name what happened, and on a throw show the text, mark it an error and toast. `DocumentReviewStatusService` owns the line and the two choose-existing dialogs, and it separates two failures the page had been treating alike: a refused precondition the user can see and fix stays silent, because the sentence is already on the step they are looking at, while an unexpected throw also toasts, because they may have navigated away by then. Separately, the per-document decision inside committing an application - which is what decides whether a regeneration spends AI tokens - was four branches in a nested chain with no test; it is now two pure functions with the staleness check passed as a thunk so the short circuit survives, and one test asserts that check is never paid for when the arguments already decide the answer. 25 new tests. `jobs.component.ts` **1226 -> 1197**; the second half of the work cost no lines at all, and an earlier attempt at it grew the file to 1204 and was correctly refused by the size ratchet.

- **Two responsibilities left the jobs page, and one of them brought a test the build used to own.** `jobs.component.ts` has been over its 400-line budget for long enough that the ratchet blocks any edit to the page, so the work is extraction before behaviour. The tailor-an-existing-cover-letter modal - its state, the base-letter read, the AI pass and the row it writes - moved to `CoverLetterTailorService` beside the `CoverLetterDraftService` it mirrors, with the base-letter read and the content assembly pulled out as pure functions so the parts worth asserting need no `TestBed`. The thirty-seven-entry icon table moved to its own module, and the spec that came with it reads the page's template, collects every `icons.<name>` it references, and asserts the table defines it: a missing icon used to pass `npm run type-check` and fail only under a full `nx build desktop`. Both extractions were checked by breaking them and watching the tests go red. `jobs.component.ts` **1467 -> 1226**; still three times its budget, with the document-drafting group named as the next seam.

- **applye.dev offers the download instead of announcing one.** The hero's primary control was a link to the documentation with a "Download: coming soon" status beside it, because the published latest release carried a single Apple Silicon `.dmg` and a Download button would have landed a Windows or Linux visitor on a page holding nothing for them. `0.29.2` is the first release with an installer for every platform, which was the flip condition written next to the flag, so the button is live. It points at the releases page rather than a versioned asset, so it does not go stale when the version moves, and the comment now records what would send the flag back: a release shipping narrower than all three platforms.

## [0.29.2] - 2026-08-02

### Added

- **The app tells you when a newer one exists, and installs it on one press.** The updater was already wired - plugin, signing key, `latest.json` endpoint - but it only ever spoke through a native dialog raised at launch, and only when an update happened to exist. So on every ordinary start it was indistinguishable from a feature that was never built, and the one time it did speak, it was a modal from the operating system across a window the user had just opened. The check now writes to signals and the result is shown where it can be acted on: a quiet dot beside Settings in the sidebar - reduced to just the dot when the sidebar is a rail - and an About block that names the running version, offers **Check for updates**, and turns into **Install & restart** when there is something to install. Every state says which one it is, including the two that used to be silent: a failed check prints the reason verbatim rather than reading as "you are up to date", and a failed install returns to the offer instead of stranding the user with no button and no explanation. The plugin sits behind an injected backend, so all seven states are reachable in a test - the previous service called the plugin directly and could only be tested by not calling it, the exact shape that made `SettingsService` a trap. 9 new locale keys in all six languages; the About privacy line stopped being hardcoded English.

- **A posting the rules cannot read is named by AI, and if that fails too, by you.** Part A made "no company found" a valid answer and rendered it as a placeholder. Part B fills it in. One press of Parse & filter now carries the whole chain: the deterministic rules first at 0 tokens, then - only when they missed - a single `job-identify` call on the economy model, then a dialog asking the user. The AI step is deliberately not behind its own button, because it runs exactly when a button would have been worth pressing, and three presses on every badly formatted posting is worse than one. The posting that defined the problem has its role in prose ("Our partner is looking for an AI-Native Software Developer") and no employer at all: it is listed by a matching platform on behalf of an unnamed partner. **Naming that platform is the canonical wrong answer** - it produces a cover letter opening "I am excited to apply to Jobgether", confidently and in the user's name - so the prompt's load-bearing rules say the company is the employer and not the board, the agency or the platform, and that a posting listed on behalf of an unnamed partner has no company. Not naming one is a correct answer. Skip is remembered on the job row rather than in the browser, because it is a fact about the job - and it silences the dialog only, not the AI, since "stop asking me" and "stop reading the posting" are different instructions; a quiet "Name it yourself" stays on the card, so skipping closes the interruption without closing the road back. What the AI named is marked `inferred` and shown with a label, never as a quotation from the posting. What the user typed is marked `user` and is **never overwritten by a re-parse** - without that, a hand-typed company goes through `is_usable_company` on the next parse and is thrown away for not looking like a company name, a validator built to reject stale garbage being unable to tell it from an employer with an unusual name. **Identification does not run inside the parse.** The parse is over when the row is written: deterministic, 0 tokens, milliseconds. Identification is a second phase - an AI round-trip bounded only by a network, then a dialog bounded by nothing but the user - and the first cut of this held Parse & filter disabled across both, which is a spinner reporting a parse that already finished and a wait that may never end. `ai_run` allows ten minutes, right for a tailoring pass and absurd for two short strings, so the phase also has its own 45-second bound. It now runs on a root singleton and survives leaving the page, the shape `WizardActivityService` already uses for the wizard's long steps: the card says `identifying...` while it runs, and a user who walked off to Pipeline gets a corner badge, not a frozen button and not a modal about a job they stopped looking at. Nothing warns before navigation, because nothing is at stake - the job row exists before the phase starts, so there is nothing to cancel. The dialog is raised by whoever is rendering the job rather than by the service, so being on screen is what proves the user is still looking. It runs behind both ways a job is made from raw text - Parse & filter and the Paste Job modal's Analyze - because they are the same event and having to find a second button is the work Analyze exists to save. The dialog also says **why** it is asking - the AI answered and could not name it, no provider is configured, or the call failed, with the provider's own error printed verbatim - because a step that fails invisibly is indistinguishable from one that was never written. Three new columns on `jobs`, one additive migration, one new command `job_set_identity` that touches the identity columns and nothing else, so naming a job cannot fork it or invalidate the score cached against its text. 13 new locale keys in all six languages. The page header follows the job now: it was pushed from the load path alone, so neither a re-parse nor naming a job by hand updated it, and it is derived from the job signal instead. `job-meta-card` and `CvPhotoPromptService` came out of the jobs page to pay for it: `jobs.component.ts` **1531 -> 1509**, its template **1156 -> 1126**, its stylesheet **985 -> 919**.

- **`libs/data` has real tests.** The layer had one spec for its entire surface, and the store is the part of it that holds state, so it is the part most able to drift. Ten cases pin what actually matters about a cache in front of SQLite: that the overview is read once and cached, that `force` re-reads, that a failed read leaves `loading` off and - the one worth having - does not mark the overview as cached, so the next attempt retries instead of leaving the screen empty until something forces a reload. Plus that a delete drops its row, a _failed_ delete does not, and a patch touches exactly one row.

- **`docs/RELEASE.md` gained a step-by-step runbook for verifying Windows and Linux builds from a Mac.** The doc already described the shape of the problem - an Apple Silicon host runs ARM64 guests, CI produces x86_64 installers for both platforms - without saying what to do about it, which left the smoke test that gates every release as a research task.
