Windows now goes through a free UTM machine running Windows 11 on ARM, which emulates x64 transparently; both the MSI and the NSIS installer get tested, because they are separate code paths with separate failure modes and both are published. Linux gets three routes with their trade-offs stated: a throwaway x86_64 cloud VM with XFCE over RDP, emulated x86_64 in UTM, or adding an `aarch64` Linux target to the release matrix, which is the only option that also gives ARM Linux users a build. Two tables that were missing and are needed at the keyboard: which asset belongs to which platform, and where the app stores its data per platform, without which the checklist's uninstall step could not be performed. The `.rpm` is named as the least-verified artifact, with the instruction to say so in the release notes rather than imply coverage that does not exist.

### Changed

- **Parsing a pasted job description leaves the page, and the page keeps the resets that were never intake's.** `JobIntakeService` (89 lines) owns turning pasted JD text into a stored job: the parse, its own progress/status/error line, the free score-cache probe that follows, and the 0-token archetype overlap check. `jobs.component.ts` **1553 -> 1532**. The method it came from opened by clearing seven signals across four different services, which is the reason this seam looked bigger than it is: only four of those signals - `parsing`, `parseStatus`, `parseError`, `archetypeMatch` - belong to intake, and the rest are the page deciding that a re-parse invalidates the job, the score, the tailoring lock and the wizard. Moving all seven would have made intake reach into `JobScoringService` and the wizard to clear state it does not own, so the service takes a snapshot in and returns a result out, and every reset that is really "the JD changed, so this is stale" stayed where that decision lives. The cache hit is returned rather than applied for the same reason. 9 tests, where the whole path had none, including the two skips that are easy to lose in a refactor: a hard-filter failure returns before the cache probe and the archetype call, and a profile with no scoring hash never touches the cache at all.

- **Saving and deleting a job leave the page, and the two busy flags become one.** `JobActionsService` (91 lines) takes the two actions that write a row and then tell the rest of the app: tracking a job as a saved lead, and deleting it. `jobs.component.ts` **1577 -> 1553**. Marking a job applied deliberately stayed behind - it commits documents, closes the wizard and navigates, which makes it orchestration rather than an action. The extraction turned up a divergence worth naming: `saveJob` and `markApplied` shared one `actionBusy` flag, and moving only the first would have given them one each, so the button that greys out during an apply would have stopped greying out during a save. The flag moved with the action and the page aliases it, which is what keeps both guarded by the same signal. 9 tests, where neither action had any, including the asymmetry that looks like a bug and is not: a failed delete clears `deleting` and closes the confirm, a successful one leaves both alone, because the caller navigates away and resetting them first puts the dialog back on screen for the frame before the route changes.

- **The link/commit lifecycle is the last of the document-drafts region, and the staleness check stops guessing at the hash.** `LinkedDocumentsService` (105 lines) owns the two documents an application points at: loading them, pointing them at a library row, committing them out of draft, and reporting whether one was built from inputs that have since changed. `jobs.component.ts` **1608 -> 1577**, and the 591-line region it started from is now four services. What deliberately stayed on the page is the decision: "is it stale, do I regenerate, which generator do I call" is orchestration, so the service only reports and commits - which is why it injects no draft service and can be tested against a fake database alone. The staleness check was quietly the most fragile thing here: it recomputed each document's input hash from a formula copied out of the generator, so the two could drift and a regenerate would simply stop firing, silently and in the safe-looking direction. Both generators now export the builder for their own hash input, and the check asks with it. 15 tests, where the whole cluster had none, including the five failure modes of a commit that is best-effort by design - already committed, nothing linked, the database throwing, the commit returning nothing - each of which must leave the draft exactly where it was.

- **Cover-letter generation follows the CV out, and the gap-fill both flows ran becomes one definition.** `CoverLetterDraftService` takes the `cover-letter-generate` call and the draft row it lands in; `jobs.component.ts` **1693 -> 1612**, against a 138-line service. The part worth naming is what happened between them: both drafts ran the same agentic gap-fill - analyse, ask, fold the answers back in, best-effort save to the profile - as two copies of the same twenty lines, with the same `finally` and the same swallowed write. That is duplicated knowledge rather than duplicated syntax, so it is now one `foldInGapAnswers` in `gap-fill.ts` (54 lines) that both services call, and `CvDraftService` shrank on the way through. 13 more tests, including the condition that was a bug once already: the cover letter skips the gap pass when a CV is linked **or still preparing**, because a CV mid-generation has not linked itself yet, and testing only the linked one let a letter started alongside it raise a second dialog for the same questions. The letter's input hash is keyed on the profile as saved rather than on the gap-augmented text - the same rule the CV draft follows - and the availability fields still go out empty, so a first letter cannot invent a notice period.

- **CV generation is the eighth seam, and the first cut into the document-drafts region rather than a move of it.** That region is 591 lines carrying three responsibilities - CV generation, cover-letter generation, and the link/commit lifecycle - so it was always going to need splitting rather than relocating. `CvDraftService` takes the first: gap-fill, structuring the tailored markdown through the `cv-import` AI skill, the date block-before-generate, and the draft row it all lands in. `jobs.component.ts` **1812 -> 1693**, against a 216-line service. The four hand-offs the cover-letter flow also uses - creating the application draft, running the gap analysis, opening the dialog, appending to the profile - stay on the page and are passed in, which is what keeps the dependency pointing one way and lets the whole pipeline be driven in tests with fakes instead of an AI. 14 tests, where none of it had any. The two pieces worth testing on their own came out as pure functions: `dateGapQuestions`, which asks only about undated entries and encodes the list and index in the question id, and `applyDateAnswers`, which routes each answer back by that id. The invariant a careless extraction would have broken is now explicit - the input hash is computed over the _tailored_ markdown, not over the gap-augmented text, so answering the dialog does not make an otherwise identical run look like a different input. Two behaviours are pinned as they are rather than as they should be: a date the parser cannot read is written to the CV verbatim, because the alternative is guessing an employment date, and a failed profile write is swallowed because the answers are already in the text being structured.

- **Wizard navigation is the seventh seam out of the jobs page, and the first one nobody was forced into.** Where the apply wizard is - open or closed, which step, and the saved progress that lets a user leave the page and come back mid-flow - moves to `WizardNavService`, taking `jobs.component.ts` from 1881 to **1812** non-empty lines against a 133-line service. What deliberately did **not** move is the work each step triggers: entering the Updated score step still auto-runs the rescore, the documents step still prepares its drafts, and continuing past the score still commits it. Those are the page's, and keeping them there is what lets the service be tested with neither an AI call nor a database - 16 tests, where none of this had any. The properties they pin are the ones that took bug reports to find in the first place: that opening the wizard over another job's unfinished session raises the confirm and leaves that session **intact** until the user answers, that closing ends the session while `forget` drops it without closing, that returning from the document editor outranks any saved progress, and that a restore is skipped for a session belonging to a different job or when the wizard is already open. `scrollContentToTop` went with them, because every one of those transitions owes the user the top of the page. Same pattern as the six seams before it: component-scoped via `providers`, signals aliased onto the service's writable signals, and `jobs.component.html` byte-identical to `main`.

- **Follow-up drafting leaves the Pipeline quick view, because the ratchet said the bugfix could not land otherwise.** `quick-view-modal.component.ts` was already over budget at 409 lines, and the job-id fix above took it to 419, so the gate refused it - the same sequence that produced `CvGapDialogService`. Drafting, its cache, the language handling and the `mailto:` hand-off move to `FollowupDraftService`, taking the modal to **306** lines against a 167-line service. Component-scoped via `providers`, signals aliased onto the service's writable signals, template untouched. The privacy guard moved with the code rather than staying on the filename: `followup-no-transmit.spec.ts` now scans the service, which is where the `mailto:` hand-off actually lives, as well as the component. 13 tests, where follow-up drafting had none, including the two properties worth keeping honest - that a space is percent-encoded as `%20` rather than `+`, because most mail clients show a `+` literally, and that the prompt receives the spelled-out language name rather than the two-letter code, since weaker models ignore a bare "uk" and answer in English.

- **Scoring leaves the jobs page, and the duplicated JSON unwrap becomes one tested pure function.** Baseline scoring, the post-tailor rescore, the deterministic ATS check and the commit-time write to `scoring_cache` move to `JobScoringService`, taking `jobs.component.ts` from 2099 to 1882 non-empty lines. The two scoring paths had each carried their own copy of the same ` ```json ` fence unwrap and `JSON.parse`, inline, inside a `try` that rethrew a truncated excerpt; that is now one exported `parseScoreResponse`, which is the only piece of scoring that can be tested without an AI call and is where the fence handling is now pinned. The asymmetry between the two scores is written down rather than inferred: the baseline is cached per profile version and reaches disk immediately, while the rescore is deliberately in-memory until the user commits, because `scoring_cache` is unique on `(job_id, profile_hash, jd_hash)` and writing it early would overwrite the "before" it exists to be compared against. One behaviour did change, and in the safe direction - `score()` guards on `job.id` instead of asserting it, so a job without an id is now a no-op rather than a row keyed on `undefined`; that removed three non-null assertions. The template is untouched, by the same signal-alias approach as the five seams before it, and `AtsService` is no longer injected by the component. 20 tests, where scoring had none.

- **The tailoring pipeline leaves the jobs page too, and the extraction wrote down two invariants nobody had stated.** The three-pass loop, its cache, its parsing and its cancel path move to `TailoringService`, taking `jobs.component.ts` from 2299 to 2121 non-empty lines. Whether a run is in flight deliberately stays in `WizardActivityService`, which already keys it by job so it survives the page component being destroyed with an AI call still in the air. The template is untouched, by the same signal-alias approach as the previous three. What the component keeps is what tailoring _invalidates but does not own_ - the export status line, the post-tailor rescore, the final checks - and those resets stay in `startTailoring` ahead of the call. Two properties were true before and are now explicit and tested: each pass's cache key covers the results of the passes before it, so pass 3 cannot be served stale after pass 1 changes, and the cache restore recomputes that same chain, which is why it stops at the first miss rather than skipping ahead. 22 tests, where the pipeline had none.

- **The jobs page starts coming apart, three services at a time, and nothing it renders changes.** `jobs.component.ts` was 2788 lines carrying roughly ten responsibilities; the three most isolated seams move out into `PortalAnswersService`, `FinalChecksService` and `DocumentExportService`, taking it to 2481. The constraint each PR was held to was that behaviour must not change: same guards, same cache keys, same prompt inputs, same thresholds, same status strings, same storage keys. The template is untouched across all three, because the component exposes the moved signals as aliases onto the services' writable signals rather than as read-only views - so the existing `finalChecksOutdated.set(!!finalChecks())` binding and every component-side write keep working against the same objects. All three services are listed in the component's `providers` rather than `providedIn: 'root'`, so one instance lives exactly as long as one page component, which is the lifetime the signals had as fields. Signal-driven throughout; nothing acquired a `ChangeDetectorRef`, so the component stays OnPush and the app stays zoneless.
- **That refactor is also the first test coverage any of that logic has had - 48 cases.** None of the three seams had a single spec, which is the ordinary reason a move like this is risky. The tests pin the things a careless extraction would quietly change: which questions reach the portal-answers cache key after trimming, that a redraft never reads the batch cache, each final-check note trigger and the 900/500-character floors, that the input hash makes a stale result outdated in one direction and not the other, and the PDF-versus-DOCX routing with the Tauri save dialog mocked. Two judgement calls are recorded rather than fixed. `portalLanguages` keeps its misleading name because the template binds it twice as the generic document-language list, and renaming it is a redesign. `commitLinkedDocument` stays inside the export `try`/`catch` via a callback, because a failure there has always been reported as `export_failed` even though the file was written - the branch is unreachable today since that method swallows its own errors, but moving the call outside would have changed the code's meaning with no test to notice.

- **The last seven page components move to OnPush change detection.** Both applications run zoneless, so a component left on the default strategy is re-checked on every change-detection pass whether or not anything it renders changed - and the largest screen in the app, `jobs.component.ts` at 2786 lines, was one of them, alongside `profile`, `settings`, `pipeline`, `apply-wizard`, `scoring-view` and `updated-score-view`. The safety condition was checked before annotating rather than after, because OnPush in a zoneless app breaks any component whose rendered state lives outside signals: all seven are signal-driven, none injects `ChangeDetectorRef` or calls `markForCheck`, none exposes a template-bindable mutable field, and none mutates class state in place. Verified in the browser as well as by the suite, since a unit test cannot catch a view that silently stops updating - rendered text on five routes is identical before and after, the theme toggle still reflows the shell, and typing into a profile field still re-renders it.

- **Nineteen files were brought up to the current Prettier, and the reason they had drifted is worth knowing.** `nx format:check` only inspects files changed against the base, so a file formatted by an older Prettier stays invisible until something touches it - and then the reformat lands as unrelated noise inside an unrelated diff. That happened twice in one week. The change is cosmetic: Prettier collapses multi-line constructs onto single lines where they now fit, which removes the optional leading `|` from union types and rejoins some argument lists, with no identifier, literal or logic change. "Whitespace only" would have been the wrong claim, because dropping a leading `|` is a token change even though TypeScript treats it as optional, so the check that actually settles it is that `nx build desktop` produces a byte-identical bundle before and after - digest `98cf308e633b84efde385a4e20771b0732c58258` across every emitted `.js` and `.css`.

- **Dev tooling moves forward as far as Angular lets it.** `@commitlint/cli`, `@swc-node/register`, `@swc/core`, `jest-environment-node`, `jest-util`, `ts-jest`, `prettier`, `typescript-eslint` and `@typescript-eslint/utils` take their bumps, along with three majors that do not care which Angular version this repository is on: `@types/node` 20 -> 26, `jsonc-eslint-parser` 2 -> 3, `lint-staged` 16 -> 17. `typescript`, `eslint`, `@eslint/js`, `angular-eslint`, `@schematics/angular` and `zone.js` are deliberately held. The grouped Dependabot PR raised `typescript` to `~7.0.2`, which nothing here can accept - Angular 21 wants `~5.9` and Angular 22's compiler wants `>=6.0.0 <6.1.0`, so nx failed to build the project graph before running a single task. Those six move with the Angular 22 upgrade, which is itself waiting on `@ngrx/signals`: its latest stable release is 21.1.1, with `@angular/core: ^21.0.0` as a peer.

- **Four of those six held packages turn out not to have been blocked at all.** `eslint` 9 -> 10, `@eslint/js` 9 -> 10, `zone.js` 0.15 -> 0.16 and `@schematics/angular` 21.2.9 -> 21.2.19 all land here. They were held because the grouped Dependabot PR could not install at all, not because any of them individually needed Angular 22 - `typescript` was the only real blocker, and grouping hid that. `typescript` stays at `~5.9.0` and is now refused by two independent constraints rather than one: Angular 21's compiler wants `~5.9`, and `typescript-eslint` 8 declares `typescript: >=4.8.4 <6.1.0`, so `~7.0.2` cannot land until both move. `angular-eslint` also stays on 21.x, and that one is genuinely gated - 22.1.0 requires `@angular/cli >= 22`.

- **Rust dependency majors, and the minimum Rust version with them.** `sqlx` 0.8 -> 0.9,
  `zip` 0.6 -> 8.6, `calamine` 0.35 -> 0.36, `base64` 0.22 -> 0.23. Two of these changed APIs
  the backend uses. `zip` split `FileOptions` into a generic type, so the docx repacker now
  builds `SimpleFileOptions`. `sqlx` 0.9 refuses non-literal SQL through a new `SqlSafeStr`
  bound, which caught four call sites that interpolate a table name; every one of them
  interpolates a string literal from an array in the same function, never caller input, so
  they are wrapped in `AssertSqlSafe` with the audit recorded at the call site. The declared
  `rust-version` moves from 1.77.2 to 1.94.0 because `sqlx` 0.9 requires it - CI already
  builds on stable, so nothing about the release path changes.

### Fixed

- **A dragged pipeline card sits under the pointer instead of chasing it.** `.card` transitions `transform` for its hover lift, and the CDK's drag preview is a clone of that element moved by writing `transform: translate3d(...)` on every pointer move - so the transition interpolated every one of those writes and the card trailed 150ms behind the cursor, leaving an echo, with the placeholder still marking where it came from. Section dragging in the CV editor was never affected, which is what confirmed the cause: `.docedit-section` has no transform transition. **The fix is a specificity fix, and the first attempt at it changed nothing.** `.cdk-drag-preview { transition: none }` lost to `.card`: both are one class, and `@use` must be the first thing in a Sass file, so everything in the extracted partial is emitted _before_ the rule it was meant to override. It is now `.card.cdk-drag-preview`, which wins on two classes regardless of order, with `.card.cdk-drag-animating` after it so the drop animation survives. The preview also gets `will-change: transform` for its own compositing layer. The guard that let the broken version through asserted that the stylesheet _contained_ `transition: none` - true, and irrelevant. It now compiles the sheet and resolves which declaration actually wins for an element carrying both classes, which fails against the version that shipped in the first attempt.

- **The shell had no test, and the update badge proved it.** The first version of the badge referenced a member the component did not have. `tsc --noEmit` does not type-check templates and nothing built `ShellLayoutComponent`, so only `nx build desktop` caught it. It has a spec now.

- **The dashboard names the job whose tailoring is unfinished, including the one you never claimed.** The "Resume tailoring for" card looked its job up in the overview list, which now holds only the jobs with an application - so a session started on an analysed-but-unsaved job produced a caption with nothing after it, an interruption that could not say what it was about. The name is resolved when the dashboard loads, not inside the `computed` that builds the card: the fallback reaches the database, and an await inside a `computed` is the same mistake that raised `NG0600` on this feature last week. It is the two-step lookup `WizardNavService.crossJobLabel` already makes - the list first, the job row second - with a third step the wizard does not need: a read that fails renders `#12` rather than nothing, because a card that cannot name its job must still say which job it reopens. Four tests build the real component and read the rendered card, driving the real `WizardProgressService`; two of them fail against the old line.

- **`SettingsService` is gone.** It held a `current()` signal filled only by `load()`, and `load()` was never called anywhere in the app, so every reader got `null` on every run - which is how a whole AI identification step silently never executed for the length of a release, indistinguishable on screen from a posting that genuinely names no employer. The 27 call sites that read settings already went through `DbService.getSettings()` and were unaffected. What replaces it is a test rather than a service: any service in `libs/data` that declares a null-initialized cache signal and hands its population to a separate `async load()` now fails a guard, because the trap is that shape and not that name.

- **A pasted job description names its company and role, or says plainly that it could not.** A posting whose header read `Company name - Elbrus` came back as "No company name found in the posting." and was titled `The Purpose:` - a section heading lifted out of the body - and neither could be corrected afterwards by any route in the app. Three causes, fixed separately. The company label was matched only as `company:` / `employer:` / `organization:` and only with a colon, so a two-word label behind a hyphen missed; labels now include `company name`, `org` and `hiring company`, and the separator may be a colon, hyphen, either dash, a pipe or an equals sign. The title fell back to "the first non-empty line under 80 characters", which is a section heading in any posting that opens with one; a candidate is now rejected if it ends with a colon, matches a known heading, or contains no word a job title is made of - **and returning nothing is now a valid answer**, because an empty field renders as a dimmed placeholder rather than a hole. And the wrong value was permanent: `job_paste` took title/company overrides that always won, and the jobs page passed the job's own stored values back on every re-parse, so re-extraction could never run. `job_paste` now takes an explicit precedence - `authoritative` for the "From link" fetch, whose values are structured fields off a job board, and `fallback` for a re-parse, where fresh extraction wins and the stored value only fills a gap. The same staleness lived one level down in the upsert, which kept whatever was already stored on a `jd_hash` conflict, so re-parsing identical text is now correctable too. Extraction moved to its own `job_identity.rs` with 18 tests including the reported posting as a fixture; `scoring.utils` gained none, because `scoring.rs` was at 647 of its 800-line budget.

- **My Jobs holds the jobs you claimed, and the badge stops calling them saved when they are not.** Pasting a description and pressing Parse & filter put the job straight into My Jobs, badged **Saved**, without the user ever pressing "Save this job". Two faults behind one symptom. The list selected every row in `jobs` with one exception carved out for Discover - but analysing writes a job row because the score cache, the tailoring and the generated documents all key on `job_id`, and that row is a working artefact rather than a decision. The exception is now the rule: the list is the jobs with an application, which is what "Save this job" and "Mark as Applied" create. The query got shorter, imported tracklists stay visible (`import.rs` writes a job and its application together), and Discover behaves exactly as before. Separately, `no_status` - the label for _no application at all_ - was translated as "Saved" in English and its equivalent in the other five locales, so a job the user never claimed was labelled as one they did. Leaving a job that was analysed and never claimed now asks first, through a guard whose whole condition is "a job is loaded and it has no application" - which covers Save, Mark as Applied and the wizard's own navigation without a special case for any of them. The abandoned row is kept rather than deleted: a first paste is identified by the text's hash, so pasting the same posting again lands on the same row and reuses the score already paid for in tokens. Deleting would charge the user twice for changing their mind. Found by reading the callers rather than by shipping it: the cross-job confirm looked its job up in the overview list, so a wizard session started on an unclaimed job would have asked the user to abandon work it could not name; it now reads the job directly when the list has no row.

- **Editing a CV bullet drew two crooked frames instead of one.** Reported from the app with a screenshot: pressing "Edit text" on an achievement line produced a heavy outer frame carrying the chip and a second, offset box inside it, while every other field showed a single clean ring. The selection highlight is one ring per selected element, and a bullet is the only leaf whose editor is **not** the selected element - the chip and the ring belong to the `<ul>`, and the textarea sits inside it as an `<li>` - so the highlight class and the editor's own focus `outline` were both painted, on two boxes separated by the list indent. The other fields never showed it because there the editor _is_ the selected element, so the two rings are concentric and read as one. Three tests, run against the old markup first, where they counted two highlighted elements instead of one. **Following the report further, editing was drawing up to three rings at once and no field was showing just one.** Every mounted editor also carried its own 2px accent `outline` at a 2px offset, so a selected leaf - the company name, the role, the summary - stacked that outside the selection ring and read as one heavy 6px frame rather than two; that is the "too thick" the report describes, and it was on every field, not only bullets. A third ring came from the app-wide `:focus-visible` box-shadow in `libs/ui/src/styles/global.scss`, which paints indigo: normally the selection ring outranks it on the same element, but on a bullet, where the ring sits on the `<ul>` ancestor, nothing outranked it - so removing the bullet textarea's own highlight exposed an indigo line inside the black one. A mounted editor is by definition inside the current selection, which is already ringed and chipped, so an editor now paints no ring of its own at all: `outline: none`, and the global focus shadow suppressed with a `:not(.cvpreview__element-selected)` guard so a selected editor keeps the one ring it is supposed to have. Every field in edit mode is now a single 2px `--cv-accent` ring with its chip, the same one the resting selection draws.

- **The CV card said "Generating" while the CV was actually waiting for an answer.** The gap dialog asks about employment dates before the draft can be written, and it is awaited inside `createCvDraft`, which itself runs inside `docGen.begin(job.id, 'cv')` with `end()` in the `finally`. So the card is genuinely preparing the whole time the dialog is up - the badge was not lying about the flag it read, it was reading the wrong flag. Nothing on screen said the CV was blocked on the user, which is why the same dialog was reported as "clicking Generate on the cover letter raises a CV dialog": it was the CV's own block-before-generate step, and #230's `(!linkedCv() && !preparingCv())` guard was holding correctly the whole time. A preparing card now reports `needs_input` while `CvGapDialogService.open` is true, in the same warning colour as `missing` and `needs_review`, and only for the CV - the dialog belongs to that flow. The derivation left the page component to get there: `documentCardStatus` is now a pure function in `apps/desktop/src/app/shared/doc-card-status.ts` over an explicit five-field state, with `documentStatusKey` beside it, so the states can be tested without constructing a 1881-line component. Nine tests, where the badge had none; the two that pin `needs_input` were run against the old logic first and confirmed to fail. `jobs.component.ts` 1882 -> 1881 and `jobs.component.scss` 987 -> 985, both still far over budget, so the fix had to shrink them to land at all.

- **The file-size gate refused every new user-facing string.** `docs/governance/CODE_QUALITY.md` excludes the translation catalogue from the budgets on the stated grounds that line count is not a design signal for it, but `tools/check-file-size-budgets.mjs` only excluded `translations/translations.ts`, a 10-line re-export. The six locale files that hold the actual strings are ~1650 lines each, over the 400-line budget, and therefore under the ratchet rule that an oversized file must not grow - so adding one key to `en.ts` was a gate failure with no legal remedy, since splitting a translation catalogue by line count is not a decomposition. The exclusion now covers the catalogue directory, minus its spec, which is what the contract already said. Found while adding the `needs_input` label above.

- **A Discover job description rendered as its own HTML source.** Reported from the app: an ArbeitNow posting displayed `<p>`, `<li>`, `&amp;` and `&nbsp;` as visible text. `strip_html` removed tags first and decoded entities afterwards, which for a feed that ships its markup entity-escaped is the worst possible order - the strip pass finds no real tags, and the decode pass then turns every `&lt;p&gt;` **into** a literal `<p>` that nothing will ever remove. ArbeitNow escapes the entities inside the markup as well, which is why `&amp;` and `&nbsp;` survived too: exactly one layer was peeled off each. Stripping and decoding now alternate, stopping as soon as a round reveals no further markup and bounded so a crafted `&amp;amp;lt;` chain cannot cost a round per layer; the ampersand is decoded last within a round, so one escaping layer comes off per round instead of several collapsing at once and inventing markup the source never wrote. `html_to_text`'s `contains('<')` heuristic is gone - it picked one of two half-treatments where the answer was to do both. **A second, older defect surfaced in the same function**: every `<` opened a tag, so a description reading "latency < 100 ms in prod" had no closing `>` and lost the rest of the line - silently, and into the very text the AI is then asked to score. A `<` now opens a tag only when a name, `/` or `!` follows it. 7 tests, each verified to fail against the old implementation before the fix was restored. **This corrects newly scanned jobs only.** Rows already stored keep their mangled text, and since `jobs.jd_hash` is the dedupe key, re-scanning a stored posting now yields a different hash and therefore a second row; cleaning stored rows needs a data repair that recomputes `jd_hash`, which is deliberately a separate change.

- **A Pipeline card's "Open full details" opened somebody else's job.** Reported from the app: clicking through from a card landed on a completely unrelated posting, which then offered "Mark as Applied" for a job the user had never applied to. `/jobs/:id` is keyed by **job**, but a `PipelineCard` is an **application** row - `db_pipeline_cards` selects `a.id` and `a.job_id` as separate columns - and the handler passed `card().id`. The two numbers collide silently whenever both tables have a row at that number, which is most of the time, so this was not an edge case but the normal path. The sibling link is the control case and is untouched: `/interview-prep/:id` really is keyed by application, so the same `card().id` that was wrong above is right there, and a test now pins both. `job_id` is nullable in the schema, so a card with no job attached is a no-op rather than a navigation to `/jobs/undefined`. The regression test was checked against the old code first, so it is known to catch the bug rather than merely to pass.

- **The follow-up draft's double-click guard never worked.** Found by the extraction below, not reported: `draft()` set its `drafting` flag _after_ `await getSettings()`, so a second click landing during that read sailed past the guard and started a second billed AI call that also wrote the cache. The flag is now claimed before the first await. Fixed rather than recorded, because the alternative was committing a test that asserts the broken behaviour.

- **A second gap dialog could strand the first document on "Generating" forever.** Reported from the app: starting a cover letter while a CV was still generating raised a second "fill the gaps" dialog for the same questions, and afterwards one of the two documents never finished. Two defects in one flow. Both document flows awaited a single `gapResolver` field, so a second caller overwrote it and the first caller's promise never settled - its `await` never returned, its `finally` never ran, and the in-flight marker for its document was never cleared. Ownership is now an explicit rule instead of whoever wrote to the field last: first caller wins, a second is answered `null` immediately, which is exactly what a cancel yields and what every caller already tolerates. The duplicate dialog was a separate miss: the cover-letter flow skips gap analysis when a CV is _linked_, but a CV that is still generating has not linked itself yet, so a cover letter started alongside one ran a second analysis. It now also skips while a CV is preparing, which is the condition the comment always claimed. The fix arrived as an extraction because the new file-size ratchet refused the in-place version - and that was the right call, because the invariant now has a home and the test seam it never had: `CvGapDialogService`, 14 tests, the collision covered directly.

- **Onboarding sent an empty model to DeepSeek, and there was no way to pick one.** Choosing DeepSeek on the AI step and saving a key still failed every call in the wizard with `The supported API model names are deepseek-v4-pro or deepseek-v4-flash, but you passed .` - reported by a user as a resume that would not parse, which is what the screen said. The step persisted `aiMode` and `provider` but never the model ids, and the dispatcher read the model back out of the settings row, so the wizard sent whatever was stored there: `claude-haiku-4-5` on a fresh install, which DeepSeek rejects, or the empty string a previous CLI-mode run deliberately leaves behind. The same class of bug had already been fixed once for the provider itself, and the lesson did not carry: for the duration of the wizard the settings row is not the truth. Settings had all the machinery to prevent this - a model catalogue, per-provider defaults, and a reconciler that repairs a blank or foreign id - but it was private to that screen, so onboarding could not use it and was free to disagree with it. It now lives in `@applye/core` as `api-models`, both screens read from it, and the wizard gained quality and economy model selects seeded from stored settings and reconciled on every provider or mode change. CLI mode still blanks the pair on purpose: a CLI rejects API model ids and picks its own default. One subtler fix came with it - the settings read is async while the step is interactive from the first frame, so a fast click now wins over the seed instead of being silently overwritten by it.

- **A spec was asserting the opposite of what the score gauge does.** `score-gauge.spec.ts` set a score in `beforeEach` and then changed it inside each band test. A change after mount is the _animating_ path - the gauge tweens over 700 ms of `requestAnimationFrame` and the colour band follows the animated value - so every band assertion was a race against that tween. It passed only because the library suites ran under zone.js, which patches `requestAnimationFrame`, while both applications run zoneless. Moving the libraries to the zoneless test environment surfaced it immediately: two tests failed because the band had not moved yet, which is exactly what a user sees. The band tests now set their score before the first effect run, which is the snapping path, so they test threshold logic and nothing else. Two tests were added rather than removed: one pins that the gauge snaps on mount instead of counting up from zero, and one covers the tween honestly by driving real animation frames until the value settles - behaviour that had no coverage at all, since the only tests touching it were asserting its opposite.

- **A dead store in the tracker print view.** `tracker-report-print.component.ts` initialised `all` to `[]` and then assigned it in both branches of the `try`/`catch` that follows, so the initialiser could never be read. Harmless, and found only because ESLint 10 promotes `no-useless-assignment` into `eslint:recommended` - which is a small argument for taking these bumps rather than deferring them indefinitely.
- **The release matrix could not build anything.** All four jobs for `v0.29.1` failed within seconds of `'nx' is not recognized as an internal or external command`. `tauri-action` invokes `tauri build` directly rather than through an npm script, and only npm puts `node_modules/.bin` on `PATH`; locally the command works because `desktop:build:tauri` wraps it, so nothing local could reproduce it. Both `beforeDevCommand` and `beforeBuildCommand` are `npx`-prefixed now.
- **The CSP guard was absent from the one path that ships bytes to users.** It runs inside the npm script and as a `ci.yml` step, and `tauri-action` goes through neither - so the release build, the only build a user ever downloads, was the only build without the check that exists precisely because a release went out unstyled. It is now an explicit step in `release.yml` ahead of `tauri-action`, and the script resolves its paths from its own location rather than the working directory. Putting the check inside `beforeBuildCommand` was tried first and rejected: it needs a relative path from whatever directory Tauri picks as cwd, and I wrote that off-by-one wrong on the first attempt - the same one that made `frontendDist` wrong for five releases.

### Removed

- **NgRx is gone, and the reason is not the one usually given.** `@ngrx/signals` was a single `signalStore` in `libs/data`, seventy-six lines holding six externally-used members, five more that nothing called, and a peer range pinning `@angular/core` to one major. It has been replaced by a plain `@Injectable` using `signal()` and `update()` - the same behaviour, the same public surface for the two components that consume it, no library. The bundle argument, which is the one usually reached for, does not hold up: tree-shaken it was a small slice of a 2.2 MB bundle and removing it saves almost nothing. The argument that does hold is coupling. NgRx's peer range would have gated _every_ future Angular major on a release of a dependency doing seventy-six lines of work, and it was already gating this one. `libs/data` also stops re-exporting `Job` and `JobOverview`, which had it republishing the domain layer's API through the data layer's entry point.
- The store's dead members went with it: `selected`, `selectedId`, `count`, `mutating` and `loadJob` were unreferenced across every `.ts` and `.html` in the workspace.

- **`zone.js` is gone, and it turns out it was never shipping in the first place.** Both applications have run zoneless for some time, and with the library test suites moved across nothing in the repository loads it. No project configuration has a `polyfills` entry, so the dependency was never bundled - its only remaining effect was to patch `requestAnimationFrame` inside three test environments, where it made a spec pass by asserting behaviour production does not have. It is an optional peer of `@angular/core`, so removing it is not a peer break. Confirmed at runtime rather than inferred: with the dependency gone the dev server boots, the app renders, and `window.Zone` is `undefined`.

- **`@angular/animations` is gone because nothing imported it.** No `@angular/animations` import, no `provideAnimations`, no `BrowserAnimationsModule`, no `animate()` anywhere in `apps/` or `libs/`. It was also the one genuinely inconsistent entry in the dependency tree: `npm ls` reported `@angular/core@21.2.19 invalid`, because `@angular/animations` had resolved to 21.2.17 and pins `@angular/core` to exactly 21.2.17. Removing the package resolves that, rather than papering over it by bumping something nothing imports.

### Security

- **Five quadratic regexes in `libs/core` no longer run on pasted text.** CodeQL flagged all five as `js/polynomial-redos`, high severity, and it was right: they parse job descriptions, CVs and model output, so their input is uncontrolled by definition. Measured on a 40 000-character pathological string, each took between 0.6 s and 1.5 s, and cost grows with the square of the length - roughly six seconds at 100 000 characters, on the UI thread, from nothing more exotic than a paste. The salary parser's percentage strip and the signature sanitiser's email pattern get bounded repetition counts (twelve digits, and the RFC 5321 lengths); a bounded email still takes the `@` and the domain with it, so no usable address survives a partial strip. The scoring-JSON fence loses a leading `\s*` that the following `.trim()` already covered. The two "trailing `(...)`" parsers behind the education and language editors drop their regex for a string scan that cannot backtrack, keeping the old behaviour on unbalanced input, which is now pinned by tests. Each of the five has a regression test that asserts a wall-clock bound on a 100 000-character input, because the failure mode is a hang rather than a wrong answer.

- **Twelve of the seventeen open dependency advisories are closed by pinning transitive versions, and the remaining five are explained rather than left silent.** None of the seventeen ever shipped: `npm audit --omit=dev` was at zero before this change and is at zero after, and the one alert with runtime scope is a Rust crate, not npm. What the advisories were doing was hiding a real one - the reason to clear them is the same reason `.cargo/audit.toml` exists, so that a _new_ finding is visible instead of lost in known noise. `axios`, `postcss`, `webpack-dev-server`, `body-parser`, `js-yaml` and `brace-expansion` are pinned through `overrides` to the patched release inside their own major. Two details were worth getting right: npm's advisory database and Dependabot's named _different_ vulnerable ranges for the same packages, so the copies that actually needed pinning were `js-yaml` 3.14.2 and `brace-expansion` 5.0.6 rather than the majors the alerts pointed at; and several advisories were backported to each release line separately, so the overrides use `@major` selectors instead of dragging a `minimatch` 3.x consumer across a major boundary. `uuid` 8.3.2 -> 11.1.1 and `@hono/node-server` 1.19.14 -> 2.0.5 follow, each a major, each taken only after the consumer was identified and the result verified: `uuid` is reached through `sockjs` under `webpack-dev-server`, which Angular 21 does not even use at dev time any more (its dev server is Vite), and `@hono/node-server` is reached through the Angular CLI's MCP server, which nothing in this repository invokes. The dev server was started and the app rendered with no console or server errors, because a passing unit-test suite is not evidence about a dev-server dependency. **Of the seventeen alerts, sixteen are now closed.** The seventeenth is `glib` and is not fixable here.

- **A clean `npm audit` was available and was refused, because it would have been a lie.** One advisory is left - `brace-expansion` GHSA-mh99-v99m-4gvg, patched only in 5.0.8 with no backport to the 1.x or 2.x lines. Forcing every copy to 5.x takes the full `npm audit` count to **zero**, and it was tried. It also breaks `minimatch` 3.1.5, which `test-exclude` and `fork-ts-checker-webpack-plugin` still carry: `brace-expansion` 5's CJS entry exports `{ EXPANSION_MAX, EXPANSION_MAX_LENGTH, expand }` instead of a bare function, and `minimatch` 3.1.5 does `var expand = require('brace-expansion')` and calls it, so both copies throw `expand is not a function` on load. Nothing in the repository catches that: with the override in place `npm audit` read 0, the entire gate went green, and even `nx test core --coverage` passed, because Jest 30 resolves its own newer `minimatch`. A pin that reads as a clean sweep would have left two live landmines behind, so the override is reverted and the advisory is recorded in `docs/governance/VALIDATION_MATRIX.md` with the exact failure and its drop condition. The full `npm audit` count lands at 23, all of it path nodes of that one advisory.

- **The `glib` advisory is Linux-only, unreachable, and not fixable here - recorded so it stops being re-triaged.** RUSTSEC-2024-0429 is unsoundness in `glib::VariantStrIter`. It is the only dependency alert with runtime scope, so it is the only one that could ship. `glib` sits at 0.18.5 because the whole gtk-rs 0.18 stack (`atk`, `cairo-rs`, `gdk`, `gio`, `gdk-pixbuf`, `gdkx11`) reaches it through `gtk` 0.18.2, which is what `tauri` 2.11.5, `wry`, `tao` and `muda` pin; `cargo update -p glib` moves nothing. Applye's Rust code never calls `glib` directly, the whole stack is `cfg(target_os = "linux")`, and `cargo audit` already reports it as an allowed unsound warning at exit 0. **Drop when:** Tauri's Linux backend moves off gtk-rs 0.18.

## [0.29.1] - 2026-07-30

### Removed

- **Two dependencies nothing imports.** `@ngx-translate/core` and `@ngx-translate/http-loader` were declared as runtime dependencies with zero references anywhere in `apps/` or `libs/` - translation has always gone through this repository's own `libs/i18n`. `zone.js` was unreferenced too and no zone polyfill is loaded by either application, so it is not a runtime dependency either; it moved to `devDependencies`, where it is genuinely needed because the library test setups still call `setupZoneTestEnv`. Migrating those setups to zoneless, so the dependency could go entirely, was attempted and reverted: it exposes two `score-gauge` tests that assert an instant colour-band change while the component tweens it over 700ms of `requestAnimationFrame`. Those tests pass today only because zone.js patches rAF and the zone test environment drains it, which means they currently agree with a runtime the apps do not have. Reworking that spec is its own task rather than a rider on a dependency clean-up.

### Added

- **The press page offers a press kit instead of asking a journalist to email for one.** `/press/applye-press-kit.zip` carries the wordmark for light and dark backgrounds, the app icon and bare symbol in both themes, three of the README's product screenshots, the hero banner, and a usage note covering the marks and what the screenshots show. It is assembled by `apps/web/tools/build-press-kit.sh` rather than curated by hand, because a zip built once by dragging files into it goes stale the first time a wordmark changes and nobody notices until a publication has already used the old one. The build is reproducible, which took three things rather than one: `-X` to drop the uid and extended attributes, a sorted file list for stable order, and pinned timestamps - a zip records the mtime of every entry, and staging into a temp directory stamps them all with the moment the script ran, so without that an unchanged rebuild produced different bytes and landed as a meaningless diff. The archive is stored in LFS on the same reasoning as the rest of the README media: it is a rebuild of assets already tracked there, so every full copy Git kept would be a second copy of the same media.

- **The READMEs answer the questions a stranger actually arrives with.** All six languages gained an FAQ - what this is, whether it costs anything, whether it applies to jobs for you, where the data lives, which AI providers work, whether it runs offline, whether it scrapes, whether it is Germany-only, which platforms - and a table naming every source Discover can read: the eleven built-in feeds with their type and market, plus the four company-board types you can add yourself. That table is the answer to a question the Features row could only gesture at, and it says in the same breath what the app deliberately does not do. "Also open source" is its own section rather than a paragraph buried under the author's biography, and a Connect section carries the maintainer's site, X and GitHub alongside a link into Discussions. `SUPPORT.md` routes a question to the right place before it becomes a misfiled issue, and an "Applye helped" issue template exists because an app that measures nothing has no other way to learn whether it worked.
- **`docs/RELEASE.md` writes down how an installer is actually produced**, including the parts that are only obvious after they go wrong: that cross-compiling to Windows or Linux from macOS is a trap rather than an optimisation, that an ARM Mac's virtual machine cannot run the x86_64 Linux artifact CI builds and what the three ways around that cost, and a per-platform smoke-test checklist to run against a draft release before publishing it.
- **Dependabot, CODEOWNERS.** Monthly grouped dependency updates across npm, cargo and the workflows, rather than a weekly per-package firehose that a solo maintainer learns to ignore.

### Changed

- **The README's media left Git LFS, which is a reversal of an earlier decision and a deliberate one.** Nine LFS-tracked files render directly on the repository's front page - the hero banner, the demo GIF, six screen captures and the walkthrough thumbnail - about 4 MB for one uncached view. The free LFS allowance is 1 GB of bandwidth a month and it does not degrade gracefully: exceed it and GitHub disables LFS downloads for the entire repository, so every image in every README breaks _and_ `git clone` starts failing, precisely while a launch is sending people to look. Plain Git blobs come off the same CDN with no metered allowance. The cost is about 4.5 MB in the pack, paid once and permanently - a fresh clone goes from 23.9 MB to 28.2 MB, and the LFS payload drops from 15.5 MB to 11.0 MB across 26 files instead of 36. The guide's screenshots and recordings stay in LFS, because Cloudflare Pages serves those out of the build and they never touch the allowance.
- **The download section points at a build that exists.** The macOS Apple Silicon `.dmg` is published, so the PLACEHOLDER block is gone from all six READMEs. Each says what is available, what is not and why, and states the Gatekeeper caveat up front rather than leaving a user to meet it as a "cannot be opened" dialog: the build carries no Apple Developer certificate, so the first launch needs right-click and Open.

- **The architecture is now enforced by the linter rather than trusted.** Every project carries `type:` and `scope:` tags, and `@nx/enforce-module-boundaries` declares the dependency stack: `core` and `ui` depend on nothing, `i18n` and `data` depend on `core`, apps depend on the layers beneath them, and nothing points sideways or upward. The graph already looked like this - the rule went in green on all six projects - but it held only because nobody had broken it yet, and "the architecture is whatever the imports happen to be" is not an architecture. `data` is tagged `scope:desktop` deliberately: it wraps Tauri IPC, the web app has no Tauri runtime, and the tag is what stops that import rather than a code review catching it. Four bare path aliases (`core`, `data`, `ui`, `i18n`) that duplicated the `@applye/*` ones were removed from `tsconfig.base.json`; nothing imported them, and an unscoped alias that can shadow an npm package is a trap waiting for a new dependency.
- **`jobs.component.ts` went from 4975 lines to 2795.** Its template and styles were inline, so the class body did not start until line 2331 - the file could not be read without scrolling past a template longer than most components. They now live in `jobs.component.html` and `jobs.component.scss`, which is what the rest of the folder already did. This is presentation, not decomposition: the class is still far too large and still owns a wizard, a scoring view and a document flow that want to be separate services. That work is scoped, not done.
- **Three more screens stopped hiding their size.** `profile.component.ts` (2665 → 833), `settings.component.ts` (1621 → 639) and `pipeline.component.ts` (1092 → 344) had the same inline template and styles that `jobs.component.ts` did, so in each case the class body began thousands of lines into the file and the component's real weight was invisible until you scrolled past it. Nothing moved between classes and nothing was renamed - this is relocation, not decomposition - but no component in the repository now conceals a template longer than its own logic, and every file still over a thousand lines is over it for a reason that only decomposition will fix.
- **`ChangeDetectionStrategy.OnPush` on 53 components** across the shared libraries, the whole website, and the desktop components that were already fully signal-based. OnPush is not Angular 21.2's default - the runtime sets `onPush` only when a component asks for it - but the framework is moving there: `ChangeDetectionStrategy.Default` is deprecated in this same version in favour of `Eager`. Eight large stateful desktop screens are deliberately excluded for now, because each mixes signals with plain fields and flipping them without a pass through the running app would trade a rendering bug for a performance win nobody asked for.
- **`docs/architecture.md` describes the layers, and two of its claims were wrong.** It advertised DOCX-first export, when what ships is PDF and the DOCX renderer is dormant in the backend; and it described a `SignalStore` per feature area, when there is one store. Both are corrected, and the layer diagram, the dependency rule, and the component conventions that the lint rule now enforces are written down beside them.
- **Working conventions became skills instead of folklore:** `.claude/skills/applye-angular` and `.claude/skills/applye-rust` state the rules for each stack - layer boundaries, component and module size budgets, state, styling and token use, i18n parity, error handling, the privacy guarantees that a backend change may not break, and the exact commands that gate a handoff. `.mcp.json` wires the first-party Angular CLI MCP server read-only, so version-specific best practices come from the installed Angular rather than from recall - which matters on a version where several defaults changed recently.

### Fixed

- **The packaged macOS app opened with no layout at all** - correct fonts, every element stacked flat against the left edge. Two settings that are each correct in isolation collided: Angular's `inlineCritical` optimisation defers the real stylesheet as `<link rel="stylesheet" media="print" onload="this.media='all'">`, parking it where it cannot reach the screen until an inline handler switches it back, while the app runs under `script-src 'self'` with no `'unsafe-inline'` - and CSP blocks inline event handler attributes. The handler never fired, the stylesheet stayed print-only, and the inlined critical CSS was the only styling that ever applied; the `<noscript>` fallback was no help with JavaScript enabled. `inlineCritical` is now off for the desktop production build, so the stylesheet loads as a plain link with nothing for the policy to block. Loosening `script-src` was the alternative and the wrong trade - critical-CSS inlining buys back network latency, and this app loads its assets from a filesystem embedded in its own binary. Nothing caught it earlier because the optimisation is production-only, the production bundle could not be built at all until the `frontendDist` fix, and CI has never reached the build step. The website is unaffected: it ships no CSP, so the same handler runs there.
- **`tools/verify-csp-compat.mjs` makes that class of bug loud.** It reads the CSP out of `tauri.conf.json` and fails the build when the emitted HTML depends on an inline handler the policy forbids, or leaves a stylesheet parked on `media="print"`. It runs inside `desktop:build:tauri` and as its own CI step. The check was verified in both directions - green on the fixed output, red with a pointed message on the broken one - because a guard that has never failed is not yet a guard.

- **The desktop app could not be bundled at all, and five tagged releases went out without anyone finding out.** `frontendDist` in `tauri.conf.json` read `../../dist/apps/desktop/browser`, which resolves from `src-tauri/` to `apps/dist/...` - two directory levels up where three were needed - so `tauri build` compiled the Rust binary, then aborted with "Unable to find your web assets" before producing an installer. The path is fixed and the bundle builds: a 16 MB `Applye_0.29.0_aarch64.dmg`, the `.app`, and the updater archive. The reason a broken build survived `v0.25.0` through `v0.29.0` is worth recording, because it is the real defect: the release workflow is refused by GitHub on billing within seconds of a tag push, so CI has never once reached the build step, and the only thing standing between a wrong path and a shipped release was a step that never ran.

## [0.29.0] - 2026-07-29

### Added

- **The README points at the tour that exists instead of the video that does not.** The walkthrough section had been waiting on a narrated 2-3 minute YouTube recording since it was written, and the README linked to it regardless. There is a six-screen silent tour of the first run published on the site today, so the section now carries a clickable poster to `applye.dev/docs/guide/tour/` - built by `docs/assets/walkthrough-thumb.mjs` from a frame of that recording, on the same backdrop as the hero banner at half the scale, with a play button, since an image that is a link has to say so. The narration script stays in the brief for whenever the narrated version is made; the poster's `href` is then the only thing that changes. With this the READMEs have no unfilled image references left in any of the six languages. The frame is the targeting step at 44 seconds rather than the obvious welcome screen, and that is a privacy decision: around three seconds in, the welcome screen's environment check renders the capturing machine's export path, which contains a real home directory name. That frame was in the shipped video; the poster does not add a second, still, indexable copy of it, and the video itself is fixed under Fixed below.
- **The README's six screenshots and its demo GIF, none of them captured for the README.** Every frame is prepared from media the documentation site already ships under `apps/web/public/guide/`, and that is the decision rather than a shortcut: a fresh set would be shot on a different day against a differently seeded database, and within a month the README and the guide would be showing two different products. `docs/assets/screens/build.mjs` picks the frame, scales to a common 1440px width and writes the six files; two of them - the pipeline board and the tailoring review - exist in the guide only as recordings, so they come out of the MP4s with `ffmpeg` at a timestamp chosen for a clean state rather than a half-rendered one. `demo.gif` is 17.8 seconds cut from three of those recordings: paste a job description, watch it parse and clear the legitimacy check, review the tailored CV and cover letter, drag the role across the board. At 800px, 10fps and 128 colours it lands at 1.9 MB, where 12fps and a full palette would roughly double it for a difference nobody reading a README will notice. The captions were rewritten to match what the frames actually show, which is not what the brief had specified: the job detail is scrolled past its score ring, Discover shows role grouping rather than numeric scores and filters, tailoring is the wizard's review step rather than a diff, and analytics shows weekly volume rather than pipeline aging. One placeholder is left in the READMEs, and it is blocked rather than pending: the walkthrough thumbnail links to a narrated video that does not exist yet.
- **The READMEs show the wordmark instead of a broken-image icon.** All six pointed at `docs/assets/brand/wordmark-light.svg` and its dark twin; neither file existed, so every translation opened with a broken image above the language picker. Both are now generated by `docs/assets/brand/wordmark.mjs`, which transcribes the lockup already in the site header rather than drawing a second one that would slowly drift from it: the same mark - the slash in the text colour, the bar in indigo - the same JetBrains Mono Medium, the same `-0.02em` tracking, the same gap, read out of `.brand` in `app.html` and `styles.scss`. The glyphs are converted to outlines rather than left as `<text>`, because GitHub serves README SVGs through `<img>`, which blocks webfonts: a `<text>` wordmark would render in whatever monospace the reader happened to have installed. The word is lowercase `applye`, as in the header; the `alt` text stays `Applye`, which is the name being read aloud.
- **The README opens with a real product shot instead of a broken-image icon.** `docs/assets/hero-banner.png` is the desktop app's Dashboard - four counters, three needs-attention cards, the upcoming interview and the recent-jobs list - captured at 2x from the running app and composited onto the brand backdrop: a graphite plate deliberately darker than the app canvas, an indigo glow behind the upper third, a faint grid, and a two-layer shadow under the window. Nothing in the frame is drawn or generated. That is the point of building it with a script rather than an image model: a hero banner is the first thing a visitor reads, and a generated interface - with its almost-words and almost-numbers - would undermine the repository on the screen where trust is cheapest to lose. `docs/assets/hero-banner.mjs` reproduces it from a fresh screenshot, and records why each measurement is what it is; the window's width, for one, is chosen so that the canvas edge falls in the gap between two list rows rather than through a line of text. It also emits `hero-banner-plate.png`, the same backdrop without the window, so the GitHub social preview and the video thumbnail do not each get their own near-copy. All six READMEs point at the banner and now describe it accurately in their `alt` text - the old wording promised a recruiter-fit score that is not on this screen. The seed persona in `docs/assets/ASSETS_BRIEF.md` was rewritten to the one actually in the frame, because the remaining screenshots have to match it or the set stops reading as one session. README media joins the guide's in Git LFS, for the same reason: these are retakes waiting to happen, and every version Git stores in full is a copy that only a history rewrite can remove.
- **The footer is three named columns instead of one wrapping row.** Eleven links sat in a single flat row that told a reader nothing about what any of them was for, and wrapped into an untidy block on a narrow screen. They are now grouped under Product, Project and Legal, with the brand, the tagline and the source pill in their own column; the raw email address became a "Contact" link that keeps the address in its tooltip. The small print underneath carries the licence on one side and the language picker and authorship on the other. The language picker is a disclosure now rather than a row of six: all six locales stay in the markup, so a crawler and a shared link still see every translation, while the footer shows the one you are reading. Every column heading and the contact label are translated into all six languages.
- **The documentation's screenshots open at full size when clicked.** The guide's figures are captured at 2880x1800 and drawn into a column a third that wide, so the badge or console line the surrounding text points at is in the file and not legible on the page. Clicking a picture - or a recording, or the enlarge button that appears on the figure - now opens it over a dimmed backdrop at whatever size the viewport allows, with its caption underneath. Escape closes it, so does the backdrop and the close button, and the page behind does not scroll while it is open. The tour video is the exception to click-to-open: it carries its own player controls, so a click there scrubs, and its enlarge button is the way in.
- **The browser tab showed the Nx logo.** The site shipped with the icon the workspace generator left behind - a blue Nx mark, 15 KB of it - as `favicon.ico`. The SVG icon beside it was the right one, but Safari and anything else that ignores SVG icons fell back to the raster file, so the tab advertised the build tool. The `.ico` is now generated from the brand mark at 16, 32 and 48 pixels, an `apple-touch-icon.png` exists for iOS home screens, and all three are linked by absolute path - the SVG link was relative, so on a page like `/docs/guide/tour` the browser looked for it under that folder, found nothing, and fell back to the Nx file even where SVG icons work. `apps/web/tools/generate-icons.sh` rebuilds them from the SVG with macOS built-ins.
- **The documentation sidebar has a landmark per section.** Quick start, User guide, Concepts, Guides and Reference each carry an icon beside their label, so the eye finds the group it wants instead of reading five uppercase words. The shapes come from the same Lucide vocabulary the desktop app uses and are inlined into the site's own icon set rather than pulled in as a package, which is how the other eight site icons already work. One icon per group and none per page: twenty-five of them in a sidebar would be noise rather than navigation.
- **The wordmark lost the blinking-cursor bar after its name.** The site's logo rendered as the mark, "applye", and a second vertical bar standing in for a terminal cursor - which read as part of the name rather than as a caret, and put two vertical strokes in a five-letter wordmark. The bar is gone from the header and the footer, and its rule with it.
- **The guide's tour video now shows the whole first run, not the first twenty seconds of it.** The old take stopped after the resume step, while the page around it promised a tour; this one runs all six onboarding screens end to end - welcome, AI setup, bringing in a resume, reviewing what was parsed out of it, confirming the suggested roles and pay range, and the summary of what finishing saves on the device - played slightly slowed so each screen can be read, with the model-call waits cut, since nothing happens on screen during them. The regenerate recording on the Profile page was re-shot for the same reason: at 2.2 seconds the working state was gone before anyone could see it, and it now shows the card compressing and then landing on its filled result. Both are silent files rather than merely muted players, along with every other recording in the guide, and the four heaviest were re-encoded: the documentation's video weight fell from about 14.7 MB to 4.1 MB with nothing visibly changed.
- **The last dashed box on the site is gone: the Discover scan is a real recording.** It is the asset that was captured once and thrown away, because scanning the built-in sources fills the feed with genuine openings from named German employers and no frame in this documentation may carry a real one. The re-shoot scans a single user-added feed of invented companies with every built-in source switched off, so the eight results on screen are the fixture's own - Northlane Systems, Kestrel Analytics, Marlowe & Fen and the rest - and nothing real was fetched. The feed had to be served over HTTPS from a public host, because Discover fetches `https://` only and trusts a bundled root store rather than the system one, so a local server cannot be scanned at all; the throwaway host was deleted immediately after the capture and the fixture stays out of `apps/web/public` so no deploy can publish fake vacancies. `guide/settings-ai.png` (API mode with Anthropic, the full privacy note, the API key block in its stored state), `guide/onboarding.png` (the AI step with both modes and the skip warning), `guide/sidebar.png` (the whole sidebar against a working Dashboard), `guide/profile-filled.png` (the contact block, two expanded positions, skills and languages), `guide/profile-archetypes.png` (three target roles, one per fit tier), `guide/score-result.png` (a genuine 72 per cent scoring run with its missing keyword, ATS check and three red flags) `guide/my-jobs-table.png` (every status and every legitimacy tier, two rows carrying real scores) `guide/analytics.png` (the counters, the funnel and the weekly chart), `guide/tracker-report.png` (the report preview in the German Eigenbemühungen format) `guide/dashboard-full.png` (four populated counters and three kinds of needs-attention card) `guide/discover-sources.png` (the sources drawer with its legality note) `guide/discover-badges.png` (feed rows with their target-role labels) `guide/discover-detail.png` (an opening full screen with its keyword-fit ring) and `guide/dashboard-empty.png` (the same Dashboard with nothing to do). Every one is captured from the running desktop app in the dark theme against a seeded demo profile of invented companies; nothing is drawn or retouched, and no key, contact or real employer appears in any frame.
- **Ten more, including the guide's first moving pictures, leaving one placeholder on the whole site.** Four stills - `guide/documents-library.png` (the CV tab with an imported CV and one tailored for a specific job), `guide/cv-editor.png` (the section stack open, with its drag handles and per-section controls), `guide/gap-dialog.png` (the wizard asking a question it cannot answer from the profile, and saying why it is asking), `guide/interview-timeline.png` (three rounds in three different states) - and six silent recordings: pasting a job description and watching it parse, importing a CV, dragging a card across the pipeline board, regenerating the scoring profile, the tailoring wizard's three passes, and the first run from provider setup to confirmed profile. Every recording is a muted looping MP4 rather than a GIF, which is what the capture rules ask for above three megabytes. Two of them are shorter than the slot they fill and their captions were rewritten to promise only what they show; `docs/product/MEDIA_SHOTLIST.md` records each shortfall with its reason. One placeholder remains, the Discover scan: the honest capture of a real scan shows real employers' postings, which no frame in this documentation may carry.
- **`tools/capture/` holds the fixtures those screenshots need**: a seed script that fills a throwaway database with the demo persona, eight jobs across every status and legitimacy tier, and their interview rounds, plus an invented RSS feed for the Discover shots. The seed writes only rows a user could have produced by using the app and refuses to fabricate AI output, so scores and tailored documents are still generated by running the real features. It will not run without `--i-know-this-wipes-the-db` and copies the target database first.

### Changed

- **The site answers on `applye.dev` and is open to search engines.** It had been live on `applye.pages.dev` since 2026-07-26 and sending `X-Robots-Tag: noindex` on every response - deliberately, because the documentation still showed dashed placeholder boxes where its screenshots and video would go, and a page removed from search results is far slower to disappear than one that was never indexed. The last of those boxes was filled, so both halves of the block came off together: the header is gone from `apps/web/public/_headers` and `SEARCH_INDEXABLE` is `true`. They are held in step by a test, so neither can be changed on its own and quietly leave a finished site telling Google to stay away. `robots.txt` allows crawling and always did, which is the point: a crawler blocked from fetching never reads a noindex header and will list the URL regardless. The apex and `www` are proxied to the Pages project, and every page canonicalises to `applye.dev`, so the `pages.dev` hostname - which cannot be removed while the project exists - does not compete with it in search.
- **The compare page states its recommendation before it shows the evidence.** It opened with a five-column matrix and eleven rows, which asks the reader to derive the answer to "which of these four" out of forty-four cells, and then repeated that answer in a paragraph underneath. The page now leads with four cards: Applye, and one for each alternative saying plainly when that alternative is the better choice and what you give up by taking it. career-ops is a link in its own card rather than a mention at the bottom. The eleven rows are still there, unabridged, demoted to a reference block under the heading that names how many differences it holds - so the claim in the cards can be checked rather than merely believed. Applye's column keeps a tint down its whole length, and the table is a real table again, with `<th scope>` on both axes: it was marked up as rows of flex boxes whose columns only lined up by coincidence of content width. Applye's card inverts to the brand graphite in the light theme, which is the strongest contrast available; in the dark theme the same inversion would sink it into the canvas, so it takes the accent border and glow the landing page already uses for the same job.
- **Every URL the site handed a crawler pointed at a redirect.** The build writes `de/index.html`, so Cloudflare Pages answers `/de` with a 308 to `/de/` - but the sitemap, the `hreflang` alternates, the breadcrumbs and each page's own `canonical` all emitted the slashless form. A crawler following the sitemap therefore hit a redirect on all 39 URLs, and the page it landed on named the redirecting URL as its canonical. Nothing errors: Search Console simply reports "Page with redirect" instead of indexing, which is the kind of failure nobody notices for a month. All four now go through one helper that emits the form the server actually serves, and six tests pin it - including one that reads the generated `sitemap.xml` back and checks it against the same helper, since the generator is a plain Node script that cannot import it.
- **`BingSiteAuth.xml` is served from the site root**, so Bing Webmaster Tools can verify ownership of `applye.dev`. It carries a verification token and nothing else; the file is public by design, which is what verification means.
- **The deploy job would have published 132-byte files in place of the documentation's media.** Every screenshot and recording under `apps/web/public/guide/` is stored in Git LFS, and the workflow checked out without `lfs: true`, so it would have written pointer stubs and uploaded them. Nothing downstream would have noticed: the build copies `public/` without looking inside it, so every gate stays green. Nothing shipped this way, because the job has never executed - GitHub Actions is unavailable on this repository - but the first run after that is fixed would have.

### Fixed

- **The tour video showed a real home directory path for the first four seconds it played.** The welcome screen's environment check renders the export folder as an absolute path, and from 2.600 s to 3.900 s of `guide/tour-walkthrough.mp4` that path carried the maintainer's account name. It was live on the documentation site. This is the second instance of the same class of leak in the same file: the July capture session caught absolute paths in the take's last half-second, after the app opened on Settings, and cut the ending - nobody then looked at the beginning. The line is blurred now, luma only so the strip stays neutral rather than picking up the green cast an all-planes blur left on the first attempt, and only for that window, because the same rectangle holds unrelated interface on later screens. The window was measured rather than eyeballed: the first eight seconds were sampled and the rectangle's standard deviation read per frame, which puts the text at 2.600 s to 3.900 s exactly. The file is re-encoded at CRF 26 and drops from 820 KB to 661 KB with resolution, frame rate and duration unchanged, so the page's dimensions and the shot list's stated length still hold. The rest of the take was checked rather than assumed: the tour ends on the targeting step with no summary screen, and the API key field visible around five seconds is the input's placeholder, not a typed key. The capture rule in `CURRENT_STATE.md` and `MEDIA_SHOTLIST.md` is widened from "Settings in CLI bridge mode" to any screen that prints a filesystem path, checked at both ends of a take.
- **A target role like "UI Engineer" matched nothing at all, and said nothing about it.** Target roles are tokenized before they are matched against job titles, and words shorter than three characters were thrown away - which is right for prose ("at", "of", "in") and wrong for the word that carries the whole role. A match also has to be anchored by a distinctive word, never by a seniority or role-family word alone, because "engineer" on its own lights up every engineering opening in the feed. Put together, "UI Engineer" reduced to "engineer", failed to anchor, and the archetype was silently dead: no target-role label in Discover, no For-you grouping, no influence on scoring prompts. Nothing anywhere said so, so the only symptom was a feed that never reacted to the role you had typed. Seven two-letter domain terms - `ui`, `ux`, `qa`, `ml`, `ai`, `bi`, `db` - now survive tokenization. `go` deliberately does not: the language is real, but whole-word matching would also fire it on "go live" and "go-to-market".
